--- riOUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. TO BUILDERS AND CONTRACTORS. The Sanitary Works Committee of the Council of the County Borough of Warrington is pre- pared to receive TENDERS for the ERECTION and COMPLETION of CONVENIENCES at Howard Place and Lovely Lane. Drawings. Specifications, &c., may be seen, and forms of tender, bills of quantities, and all further in- formation obtained at the Office of the Borough Surveyor, Town Hall, on and after Wednesday next, the 24th inst., at which place tenders must be delivered on or before 12 o'clock noon on Wednesday, the Ist day of July. The lowest or any tender not necessarily accepted.

ANDREW M. KER. Borough Engineer & Surveyor. Borough Engineer & Surveyor's Office. 19th June, 1914.

REQUIREMENTS.

The Sporting LOUN 6 E

NEVER has a gentleman's fashion attained such Popularity as the easy Norfolk Coat. The old- fashioned Norfolk had a reputation for ease quite its own, but even in this partic- ular it cannot be compared to its modern rival. For golf this is the only possible cut. The inverted pleats on the shoulders give to the movements of the body an easy swing which is not possible with other makes.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR ANNUAL REST.

Continued from Page 2.

W. KNIGHT, STOCKTON HEATH. To lovers of Nature, a few words of practical common-sense advice appears in the advertise- ment of Mr. W. Knight, London-road, Stockton Heath. Cyclists essentially are birds of pas- sage, for "cycles are most adaptable to tourists for leaving the beaten track and going " where idle fancy leads." The charm of seeing the best of Nature and the open country is, however, com- pletely spoiled if one has a lack of confidence in the reliability of the machine, and a thorough overhauling in the hands of an expert—which, after all, only means a trifling cost—obviates all the dangers, disappointments and disasters at- tendant Upon a holiday tour on a machine only ill fitted up. Better be safe than sorry—see W. Knight now, and take no risks.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON TAXI -CO.

"Make your own time-table" is the advice of Mr. W. J. Lake, of the Warrington Taxi Cab Co., Lion Hotel. Everybody knows the annoy- ance one experiences when having to rush for a train at a fixed time—usually just as one has found an ideal spot or is in the midst of en- joyment. Now this is altered—individuals or parties can make their own times and terms. Beautifully, equipped touring cars and chars-a- bane can be hired at prices which compare well with rail fares. A holiday tour can be arranOd and prolonged or shortened at will. No hurry, worry, bustle or annoyance. As EO aptly stated in the advertisement: "Go as you please—where you please—how you please—and when you please."

===== 0002601_19140620_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
MADAME LILLIE.

The name Madame Lillie is now -so well known as to render comment almost superfluous. This establishment ,at 13, Rylands-street, has firmly,, built up a reputation with the public of War- rington by strict attention to the wants of every customer, constant study of the newest styles in all that is latest and best in Millinery, and.by giving value—sterling value. The approach of the holidays and Walking Day has been antici- pated, and the . newest and most charming styles, fashions and shades are now being shown. The last word in ladies' wear is to be found here: Millinery of merit, dainty and durable Blouses, lace, fancy neckwear, feathers, flowers, ribbons and ornaments. See our windows, or better still, see our showrooms.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
MISS FLEWITT.

The rapid success of Miss Flewitt, O. Bridge- street, conclusively proves that the ladies of Warrington are quick to discern style and taste. The art of millinery at its best is seen in these showrooms. Immediately fickle fashion changes. the very latest models and creations from the London and Paris houses are exhibited. The Prices are in no way prohibitive, and ladies desiring distinctive "style" and " tone " may depend upon having their most exacting de- mands satisfied by Miss Flewitt and her staff of talented assistants. •

===== 0002601_19140620_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR ANNUAL REST.

Continued from Page 3.

with one or more articles short—the poor man was in a state of collapse. • Of course, there are still many people who prefer to take their luggage with them, and even luggage in advance does not always arrive when it should, and the railway companies come in for a large amount of abuse. They are not, however, invariably to blame for the delay and wrong sending of luggage. Often—one might say more often than not—the real fault rests with the sender. Badly written or incorrect addresses, carelessly attached labels, are the fre- quent cause of delay and loss of baggage, but the most prolific source of trouble is old labels and addresses. There are indivinals who never take the trouble to remove old addresses, and there are others who take a pride in getting their bags and boxes covered with testimonies of their globe-trotting propensities. Picture to yourself, then, the dilemma of a porter at a junction station confronted with a piece of lug- gage bearing two or three addresses to various parts of the country, and perhaps a dozen rail- way and shipping labels to other spots more or less familiar to him. What can he do? What he actually does is to choose the newest-looking label and send the luggage accordingly. The true solution is for people to take greater care of their own property. If every traveller this summer took the trouble to wash all old labels off and place two labels on each of his packages with his address clearly written upon them, the railway companies' difficulties would be far leas, and the improvement in the temper of travellers would be remarkable. The reason for placing two labels on your packages is that railway porters. are not per- fect, and there are a few, perhaps, who might tear off the address tag. In that event the second label is 'invaluable. Then, of course, in a crowded luggage van often only one end of a package is visible, and if it happens to be the unaddressed end, overcarrying and consequent delay may result, or contact with other pack- ages may remove a label, and if that label is the only one the chances of the package reach- ing the correct destination are somewhat remote. A good idea also is to place an ad- dressed label inside the trunk on top of the con- tents. The custodians of lost property open each piece of luggage, and an address inside ex- pedites its restoration to the sorrowing owner. A last hint to travellers, and one which may save some a lot of worry and loss, is never pack money and jewellery in your trunks, unless you are going to insure them. An Act of Parlia- ment known as the Carriers' Act relieves rail- way companies from liability for damage or loss ~ of such valuables where their value exceeds £lO, and many a person has been astounded after having lost jewe llerv, money, silk goods, and other articles speoa • lly mentioned in the Act, _ by the company upon whom they ye have politelye d ia itnio led that no compensation can be paid.

SEVEN KILLED BY LIGHTNING.

One of the most violent summer storms that has ever been experienced in England raged over London on Sunday. It was remarkable alike for its intensity and duration, the eccen- tricity of its path and the devastation that it wrought. On Wandsworth Common six persons—four of them young children—were killed by lightning and several others, one of whom has since died, were injured. Enormous damage was done by hail and Hoods.

BUILDING SOCIETY. Further Success Reported at Annua Meeting. FOUR PER CENT. DIVIDEND.

A successful year was reported at the 44th annual meeting of the Warrington Working Men's Permanent Benefit ,Building Society, which was held in the Golborne-street school- room on Monday evening, when Mr. W. H. Wal- lington occupied the chair.

Annual Report. The Secretary, Mr. Peter Dalton, read the following report for the year ending 30th April, 1914 : " During the year the sum of £1,814 14s. 6d. has been received as subscriptions; £3,603 19s. 6d. for repayments; and £7 19s. 6d. en- trance fees, fines, &c.; and bankers' interest, £4l 13s. 5d., making the total receipts £5,468 6s. 11d.; the working expenses being £B3 Os. 2d. The withdrawals amount to £950 6s. 6d., whilst the sum of £2,075 has been advanced to members. There are 240 subscribers, holding 1380 shares; and 58 borrowers, holding 216 f shares; making 298 members, holding 596 i shares. The bonuses this year amounted to £363 2s. 6d. on the amount lent, of which sum ..t66 Bs. is divided as profit, and £296 14s. Gd. carried to the prospective interest account. The total amount remaining owing upon mortgages is £11,539 17s. 4d., of which only £10,202 3s. 3d. can be regarded as capital, the balance of £1,337 14s. Id. representing the proportionate amount of interest thereon, calculated for the number of years the several mortgages have still to run. The profits for the year, including £3 7s. 7d. from last account, after deducting working expenses, amount to £499 6s. 7d., out of which the managers recommend a dividend of 4 per cent., which will take .t 469 lie. 6d., leaving a balance of £29 15s. id., £25 of which they direct be placed to the credit of the contingent fund, and the balance .24 15s. id. carried for- ward to next year's account. The total amount owing to the society on mortgages is £11,539 17s. 4d. The valuation of the property upon which the advances have been made is 1'23,075. The whole of the managers retire. Messrs. Harding, Hope, Baxter, W. H. Wallington, William Charnock, and J. W. Parker are eligible, atilt offer themselves for re-election. Six additional managers will be taken from the register in rotation." iTre.h crlt er erilieoprehi?. s otrt ueaa.n d balance sheet were passed, on t a proposition of Mr. George Hill, seconded by The Chairman sai:d 4 p er h e would be paid for the year's dividend.

Officials. The retiring officers were re-elected, on the motion of Messrs. T. F. Wallington and Grealey, Messrs. Robert Lee and T. F. Wallington were re-elected surveyors, on the motion of Messrs. 11. Hope and W. Booth. Messrs. John Green Chappell and George Armstrong were re-elected auditors, on the motion of Messrs. Parker and Charnock. It was resolved that one shilling he paid to each manager for his attendances during the past ye.ar, on the motion of Messrs. Grealey and Pennington. Messrs. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 20, 1914.

SONS OF CAMBRIA,

First Social of the New Warrington Society.

WALES AND EDUCATION.

Thoughts of the part Wales has taken, is I taking, and intends to take in the development of the Empire of which she is proud to belong, were suggested on Monday evening by the large assembly of Welshmen—and Welsh women—at the Winmarleigh Cafe. The air .was full of melody—vocal and instrumental—fittingly giv- ing the finishing touch to a gathering composed of those who, directly or by descent, come from the Land of the Mountains, in which the secret spell of music appears to be an inherent virtue. The occasion was the first social held under the recently inaugurated Warrington Branch of the Welsh National Society.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
PLEASANTRIES IN WELSH.

To the " foreigner '-' the ..ree;in—g—;N—oswaith Dda " ("Good Night") sounded something un- canny, but the cheery faces and the hearty hand- shake made one feel that the sons of Cambria were retiable, jolly good fellows. The chair was occupied by Councillor Roberts, who was introduced to the audience as the first president of the local branch by Mr. E. Wynne , Jones, who said, first in Welsh and then in English, that he had Pleasure in introducing one who was known to all Warrington—(hear, hear) —and one who was greatly admired, but not more so than by the Welsh patriots in the town. (Hear, hear.) His heart, if not his held— (laughter)—was very warm towards all things that belonged to the Welsh nation. (Applause.)

"TO KINDLE AND CREATE ENTHUSIASM." After several musical items, Mr. WYNNE JONES, addressing the gathering as " Foneddigion a Foneddigesan" (ladies and gentlemen), said that one of the first and most important objects of a Welsh National Society was to kindle and create an enthusiasm and an interest in all that referred to the Welsh national life. That, of course, was a very important factor in the creating of all that was national, because love of Welsh national life had to do with the forma- tion of that which went to make true national- ists. Another object was to greate an interest in Welsh literature, to study the history of Wales, its language and customs, and so obtain a true conception of the history of their country. They had put that in the forefront of their programme, because they believed that it was necessary to study the history of Wales, and especially its customs and literature, to get into their hearts the true national spirit, and to get a true conception of what it was to love one's country. They could not possibly love their country n,nless they knew their country, and one of the principal objects of that society was to know the past, because the past bad so much to do with what Wales was to-day. They were proud of their president as a Welshman, but although Councillor llobeits would 136 an excellent president lie would be much better if he could address them in their native tongue, and, write a Welsh letter to them occasionally. One of their objects was to do that; to learn the , language in a conversational and clean way, and speak it in a simple manner. •

===== 0002601_19140620_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
Wales to the Fore in Education:

They wanted to create a spirit of love for their country. (Hear, hear.) " Do you know what has made it possible for us to take an interest in all that is Welsh?" asked Mr. Jones. "It is because we do love our country. We do not only love it because of its hills and valleys, but because of the place it has taken in the formation of character and because it stands very much in the forefront of all nationalities in respect of education--elemen- tary, secondary and higher." (Applause.) Wales, in all parts of the world, was admired because of the interest its sons and daughters had taken in the question of higher education. Another idea of the society was to remove the bar- riers, which kept people away from one another, and there were many barriers of all kinds—and to replace those barriers to unity. Welsh unity was absolutely essential—(hear, hear)—and they wanted to bring all in, just like the Welsh National Eisteddfod, which knew nothing about politics or creed. If a man was connected with Wales through marriage or otherwise he would be welcomed in that society. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

"MY FELLOW-COUNTRYMEN." Councillor ROBERTS, addressing the audience as "My fellow-countrymen," said he could not speak Welsh. If he had known, when he under- took to occupy that very honourable position, which he appreciated very highly, that it was considered a qualification for the presidency, that the Welsh language was to be learned, he would have considered a little longer before responding so quickly as he did. (Laughter.) However, he possessed one of the essential quali- fications, and that was he had a Welsh. name— (laughter)--and he was not ashamed of it. (Applause.) On his father's side he had in- herited some of the Welsh characteristics. Welshmen were thorough in all they did. He could claim kinship on his father's side, whose memory he held in great respect, because he taught his family two or three truths. The first was a reverence for Sunday, and the second was the value of education.

Welsh Educational Ideals. He thought the Welsh people had a higher ideal of education than any other people of the British Isles, perhaps with the exception of the Scotch. The English people were just begin- ning to waken up in the matter. In Wales they had had a system of secondary education far superior to that which has obtained in England until now, and that in Wales had been largely assisted by orking men. Wales was a little country—but a beautiful one; he could not understand any man with a Welsh name com- ing back from such a country without feeling proud.

"Done Great Things." It was a Poor country, but out of its poverty , it had done great things. In the world of art, in science, in law, in philosophy, aye! and even in statesmanshiP, Wales had given of her best, and that best had had a great deal to do with the framing of this country. (Hear, hear.) In the Army and the Navy Welshmen had always done their Part. There was an additional object he would suggest should be added to those of the society, and that was they should give kindly and brotherly advice to any, Welsh- man who needed it, and even material advice, if necessary. (Hear, hear.) Concluding, Councillor Roberts said he warmly appreciated the honour they had done him, and if he could be of any service whatsoever it would be a great pleasure to him to render that ser- vice. (Applause.)

A SUGGESTION FOR ST. DAVID'S DAY. The Rev. F. E. EVANS, of Appleton Thorne, in proposing a vote of thanks to the artistes and president, suggested that the Rector of War- rington be approached with a view to having a large Welsh service in the Parish Church on St. David's Day.

- The Rev. Father PRIMAVESI seconded the pro- position, which .was supported by the Rev. R. PARRY JONES.

The CHAIRMAN, in replying, said that meeting would leave pleasant and happy memories behind it, but if the society was to he the suc- cess they desired they should be determined to rope in all in the district who had any Welsh blood in their veins. (Hear, hear, and ap- plause.)

THE ARTISTES. . A most enjoyable programme was sustained by the following artistes: Missed Freda Holland (harpist), Birkenhead s Williams, Widnes; Mary Brundett, Lymm ; Elsie Hewitt and 'Winnie Jones, Mrs. W. T. Williams, and Mr. Willie Hughes, of Warrington. - The majority of the songs were rendered in Welsh. There are already over 50 members in the Warrington branch of the society, and the num- ber is steadily growing. The Rev. F. E. Evans (Appleton) and Messrs. E. Lloyd Jones and W. T. Williams have been appointed vice-presidents.

4.401444010,1444,101014614+44444444•4. POULTRY PEN. 4$ Hints and Interesting Facts and Chat for Amateurs. 114014.14,14.14+014.1014014.1.44+.14,1444.1401.

Although the statement often appears to the contrary, it is nevertheless a fact, says an ex- pert, that a comfortable living can be made from poultry. This is evident to anyone who cares td take the trouble of making systemat inquiry. and searching One or two concrete examples may, however, be given, since an ounce of actual proof is worth a ton of theoretical reasoning. A woman who started poultry keep- ing in quite a small way in the West of Eng- land about ten years ago is now making and has been making for some seasons past, an average of over £2OO per annum. Her best season the , profits amounted to within a few pounds of £3OO, while at no time as far as I am aware, during the last five years has her income been less than , A man in the North of England has supported , himself, his' wife and three children for several years, perhaps not in luxury, but, at all events, in comfort. A lad of about 23 years of age, re- siding in the South of Ireland, has maintained himself for some Years f)ast upon the proceeds of four and a *half acres, during which time he has saved a considerable sum of money; while a young married woman, whose husband met with an accident and was thereby rendered useless for life, has supported herself, her husband, and two children for over three years. Examples of a similar nature could be multiplied, but enough has been said to show that when fowls are pro- perly looked after, and when other essential fac- tors are favourable, they are capable of yielding a generous profit..

UTILITY POULTRY CLUB'S COMPETITION Great heat for the tinae of year, resulting in broodiness, and in some cases in moulting, is one of. the chief points to which the manager of the Twelve Month' Laying Competition at Sedlescombe draws attention in his report for the seventh month. The.effect of the unusual heat has been more felt by the heavy breeds than the light breeds, and as a consequence the latter have in many cases improved their posi- tions, though the leading pens remain the same as at the end of last month. The total number of eggs laid during the month by the 64 pens of four birds each is 4,860, or 53 eggs more than the previous month. The average per bird for the 32 pens in the small house section working out at 19.87 eggs per bird, and for the 32 pens in the semi-intensive sec- tion at 19.31 eggs per bird. The leading pen (white Leghorns) in section 1 has now a total of 512 eggs (value £3 2s. 5d.) to its credit, and the leading pen (white Wyan- dottes).in section 2 has laid a total of 505 eggs (value £2 18s. 10d.). The positions and scores of the leading pens are as follows :

RAILWAYMEN'S HOURS.

The National Union of Railwaymen have this week passed resolutions in favour of (1) the principle of an eight-hour day, or forty-eight- hour week, for all railway workers; (2) of the Enginerrien's Certificate Bill: and (31 of a Bill to teekire the right of trade union representation at inquests.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

HAY-MAKING HINTS.

With the hay-making season upon us, it is not out of place or time to express the hope that the crop will be a satisfactory one, and that the atmospheric elements will be kind. The best time to cut hay is when the bulk of the grasses are in flower—that is to say, when the anthers or pollen-sacs are protruding from between the chaff-scales: It would appear that this is the time when the weight of herbage is nearly at its maximum, while the nutritive and digestive pro- perties are at their best. It is also, I think, ..worth mentioning that in the case of meadow hay the crop can hardly be cut too close to the ground. One is aware that some farmers hesi- tate to over-cut their meadow hay, but I think. they should take as much off the ground as they possibly can. With rotation hay, however, it is found that red clover produces more aftermath if the first crop has not been cut too close, and especially so if very sunny weather occurs when the crown of the root has recently been exposed. A further point of importance is as to the time for " making" the hay; and here, as every farmer is aware, it is as well to rely on the state of the weather as the main factor for deter- mining when the hay shnll be aCtually made. It may, however, be said that some farmers turn their hay too much, the result being that there is a shedding of seed, ,which ought to be pre- vented. In the case of clover this is very serious. Consistent with removing the hay in a good condition, and without rain upon it, it should be turned as •little as possible.— "Ranger," in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19140620_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPROVEMENT IN PASTURES AND GREEN CROPS.

Broken weather was general last week through- out the country and rain fell in all parts, although in irregular quantities. The thunder- storms, though local, were extensive and satur- ated the ground thoroughly. Some districts, , however, had only drizzles, refreshing the sur- face, but of little effect., Correspondents in the ' upper Thames valley state that the rainfall there up till the middle of the week was insigni- ficant, and similar reports come from the East Midlands and parts of the counties south of the Thames. These reports, however, were made before the thunderstorms which lasted through- out most of Sunday. In Lincolnshire and all over the North and Midlands to the Severn Estuary there were fine soaking rains, a 12 hours' downpour in Warwickshire, and a rain- fall of almost equal duration in Gloucestershire having completely removed anxiety and imme- diate inconvenience. The prospect can be described as greatly improved and is particularly reassuring north of the Trent and in Lincoln- shire, except for the injury done by the frost to the potato crop.—" The Times."

===== 0002601_19140620_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
CROP AND STOCK PROSPECTS.

The crop reporters of the Board of Agricul- ture, in reporting on agricultural conditions in England and Wales on the Ist June, state that wheat is very generally a good plant and promis- ing, and appears to be the best of the three cereals. Barley and oats are less satisfactory, and the later sown corn especially is often a thin plant. Early potatoes have throughout the whole country, except in favoured situations, been cut by the sharp frosts at the end of May, and much damage has been done to them, while a serious consideration in their case is also that the date of lifting will be delayed. Mangels, where up, appear to be a fair plant generally, though often irregular, and nearly everywhere backward. Strawberries appear about average, on the whole—though very different in different districts; raspberries a little over average, cur- rants and gooseberries average. Apples and cherries are over avlvage, particularly in the southern counties; pears about average, and plums rather below. Some notes will be found in the divisional reports concerning the progress and condition of crops grown for seed in the eastern counties, such as turnip and mangold seed, as well as mustard. The prospects for both kinds of hay are poor; the grass is in want of rain, and the cold nights have retarded growth.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

To obtain fine blooms of Sweet Peas remove all side growths and feed with liquid manure when the buds are opening. Briars growing as standards for budding pur- poses generally throw out more shoots than are required. Cut off all but three or four at the top. By July, which is the budding month, the growths will have reached a nice length. Prime flowering Currants rather hard. The result will be long shoots for flowering next year. If more Chrysanthemums are wanted, tha sturdy growths at the base of the old stools will root readily now, and furnish bigger blooms than the parent plants. As soon as Sweet Peas become streaked com- mence spraying with a dilute solution of Per- manganate of Potash.

THE GOOSEBERRY CATERPILLAR. A correspondent says that Gooseberry bushes may be saved from caterpillars by picking off the leaves with a scarlet blotch on. These blotches, it is pointed out, gradually develop into eggs, from which the young caterpillars emerge.

• To destroy Gooseberry caterpillars get a quan- tity of Elder leaves (writes W. Putman, in "Garden Life"), cover with water and boil till the liquid is black. When cool, strain, and to every gallon of this add one gallon of tobacco water, the colour of strong tea. Water the trees when they are dry with this solution, which will cause the caterpillars to fall down. • An excellent method of conserving moisture in the soil where seedlings are is to scatter lawn mowings over the beds. The seedlings will come on more quickly.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
VEGETABLE HINTS.

Broccoli may be planted out where early Potatoes or Peas are now growing. The Cauliflower is an annual, and therefore more tender than the Broccoli, whieh is very hardy. The heads of the Cauliflower are a dense mass of flower buds, while the Broccoli flowers are more open.

There are varieties of Broecoli which sprout, providing a number of branching flower heads. These plants may be left in the ground to pro- vide further flowers in successive seasons. In lifting Cabbages, Cauliflowers, Broccoli, Sprouts, &c., from the seed bed, use a trowel. The plants must he lifted without injuring the roots and planted with the trowel, spreading out the roots as much as possible. Press the soil firmly to the roots. Liquid manure must only be given to plants when the soil is moist.

_ To have nice crisp Lettuce it must be grown in a friable soil, liberally manured, and kept well watered.

A BEAUTIFUL DWARF PHLOX.

One of the most attractive herbaceous plants at the present time is Lapham's Phlox, P. divaricata Laphamii. .The soft blue flowers are borne in great profusion from one foot to 18 inches high, and it is worthy of a place in all gardens. Grown in a mass, it reminds one of a large cluster of ' blue Plumbago.—" The Garden."

===== 0002601_19140620_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
VASES AND BOXES.

In the making up of these useful adjuncts to the flower garden it must be remembered that as their season is so very 'short and the space limited, none but good established plants should be used. Manx fine effects can be had by making use Of goal-sized plants of some of the hardier varieties of Fuchsias as a centre plant, while the rest of the vase or basket could be filled with smaller-growing plants, such as Ivy-leaved Pelargoniums.—" The Garden."

THE ROYAL SHOW.

Bright Prospects for the Shrewsbury Meeting.

The Royal Show at Shrewsbury will be one of the most imposing of the long series of exhibi- tions promoted by the Royal Agricultural Society. With one exception it surpasses all its predecessors in extent, and in its range of inter- est for the practical agriculturist it will cer- tainly challenge comparison with any other show which has ever been promoted. But this year's meeting is peculiatly favoured in its extrinsic features, the features which it does not take the eye of an expert to appreciate. The show- yard is pitched on historic ground in the en- chanting ,surroundings that only an old town which has played a -leading part in the evolu- tion of a nation can boast. Shrewsbury sits enthroned above the Severn within hail of the temporary timber and canvas city of the plain. To reach • the showyard the visitor will pass through scenes which speak, often in accents grim enough, of the making of England from the time of the barbarian founders of the race onward. -

Holiday-maker Attractions. From the standpoint of the farmer as well as the holiday-maker on pleasure bent it is an ideal situation for a show, a meeting-place for East and' West, North and South. There is always a commingling of .tongues at Shrewsbury to remind one of its proximity to the Welsh Border, and this will broaden into a more com- prehensive cosmopolitanism during the first week in July. The show opens on the last day in June and continues till Saturday, July 4th.' Shrewsbury is in touchi with numerous popu- lous centres, and it s hoped and expected that the attend,ance will eclipse past records as the entries and the value of prizes have done.

Science on the Farm. The cities can hardly affect to be indifferent to the progress of an industry upon which our food supplies so greatly depend, and there is a reciprocity of interest in the increasingly im- portant part which the manufacturer is called upon to play in agriculture through the appli- cation of mechanical inventions to many of its branches. The implements and the multifarious accessories which are being sent to this year's show will interpret to all who are interested in such matters many of the secrets of up-to-date farming. Such exploits of ingenuity as milking cows by machinery have almost lost their novelty for bucolic moderns. Thousands of visitors to the Royal will be interested, however, to see how, the miracle is wrought. The town dweller hardly appreciates how busy science has been with the affairs of the farmer. Shrews- bury Show will afford him a unique opportunity of learning all that there is to be learnt on the subject.

Record Entries and King as Competitor. Coming to the various sections of live stock, every main department contributes to swell the total to the record figure of 3,394 entries. North Country lovers of horseflesh will be gratified by the fine show made by their favourite breeds, and the spirit with which exhibitors whose names are familiar in their mouths as house- hold words, are bidding for fresh, laurels. Southerners, too, will find plenty to stir their local patrietism. For the first time the King is Showing in the class for Cleveland bay or coach. horse, three animals having been entered from the Royal Mews His Majesty also sends the pick of his flocks and herds at Windsor and Sandringham. In each of these departments he will be challenged in friendly rivalry by his subjects of every degree from the peer to the yeoman. Shorthorns are as strong numerically as at the Bristol meeting last year, the dairy class beine4raore predominant on the present occasion. Breeds which are distinctively asso- ciated with the Midland and Northern districts are very well represented indeed. A great show of all breeds of dogs will be an added attraction this year, and other special features which have been introduced will mate- rially strengthen the appeal of the Royal to the populace. Sir Gilbert Greenall is again the Honorary Director of the show.

National Union of Clerks caters for all of clerical workers. That union, we

know, plead, demand—aye, enforce those con- ditions of labour which should be the birth- right of every clerk who has no desire to eat of a mess of pottage. The numbers of that union are leaping up, their organisation is being per- fected, the' are throwing off the yoke of snob- bishness by drawing nearer to kindred unions, and they are outlining a new bold programme of clerical reform. They are a body of men who are changing the whole tone of clerkdom' for having gained self-respect by' organisation, they are also respected by their employers. It is the funk who "bums" that is of no consequence either in the eyes of his fellows or his employ- ers. Employers of clerks may get them " any- where at a pound a week," but if those clerks would only organise their forces, if they would but formulate their demands, and fight their battle with such weapons, industrial and per- haps political, as are readiest to hand—then there would be a greater change. But this much is certain, the clerk-e organisa- ..

tions have come to stay, they are growing in manhood, dignity and organised power, and will scorn more and more the rags and poverty of clerkdom. We consider that the clerk who remains outside his union is not only doing an injustice to his fellow- worker, but is slowly murdering his family and committing slow suicide by allowing his life's blood to be sucked from him—without eiren making a fight. Therefore clerks do not v.-aste-your time. Why lose your chance? Why vex your soul? Why ruin your hopes? Why anger your wife? Why starve your bairns whilst' this organisation for which you have grumbled and of which you have dreamed is at your elbow, ready to back you? More power to it; stumble in ! Bungle in! Join the Clerks' Union—you want it—it wants you.

F. W. R.

P.S.—All information concerning the Clerks' Union.inay be obtained from Mr. W. Palin, 3, Fitzherbert-street, Warrington.

PRAISE FOR THE INFIRMARY.

WARRINGTON MUNICIPAL BOWLING

GREENS' LEAGUE.

Sir,—.ldverting to the letter with reference to the above which you kindly published in your paper; I am pleased to say that, as a result, a meeting of representatives from the various municipal greens was held in the Royal Oak Hotel, Bridge-street, on Monday, when a League was formed, consisting of the following greens, viz., Liverpool-road, St. Elphin's, and Latch- ford Nos. 1 and 2. The following officials were appointed : Chair- man, Mr. J. E. Eaton, St. Elphin's green; hon. secretary and treasurer, Mr. John H. Knowles, Liverpool-road green. The first two matches were arranged to take place on Wednesday, the 24th inst. as follows : Liverpool-road v. Latchford No. 2. Latchford No. 1 v. St. Elphin's. As an average of 60 members per green jianded in their names, it was decided that each -team should be composed of 24 players 12 to play at home and 12 to play away on the same evening.

JOHN H. KNOWLES. 49, Samuel-street, Warrington, 18th June, 1914.,

===== 0002601_19140620_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
LYME COUNCIL AFFAIRS.

Sir,—Has not the time arrived for the rate- payers of Lyinm to take rather more interest in their affairs than heretofore? -Particularly I refer to the acts of the Urban District Council as at present constituted. For instance, there are the proposals connected with the gas under- taking. Do the ratepayers endorse the action of their supposed representatives (mostly self- elected) in this matter? The time which hag. been wasted on childish talk about "trippers' might more profitably linve been spent upon finding a solution to the many difficulties which at present face the Council, such as obtaining something like value for the high rate with which we are now threat- ened. It will no doubt be some satisfaction to those of the virtuous members who object to other people drinking beer to know that the reports of the " tripper" agitation had the effect of cancelling at least one trip to Lymm, something like 250 people being concerned. No wonder fewer of the general public visited the village during Whit-week. I trust that our influential ratepayers will come forward and express their opinions on Council matters generally. Lymm, 18th June, 1914.

WORKER.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
DOMESTIC SERVICE & SWEATED LABOUR

Sir,—Will you kindly insert this letter in your very interesting paper? I have been reading lately about sweated labour done by women. I think domestic service is the most sweated of labour. lam a servant myself, and I know how the poor servant is overworked. I am on my feet from 6 a.m., until 11 p.m. The servant is never done. _

Why do not servant girls form a union for an eight or nine hour day? I would gladly join. Mistresses are never satisfied. If our esteemed Member for Warrington would speak in the House of Commons on the servant girl question and get a Bill passed for a nine- hour day, he would be doing a noble act for the downtrodden servant. No one seems to tackle the servant problem. A servant ought not to be made to wear a cap if she objects to it. She ought also to be addressed as Miss or Mrs., not Mary Ann or whatever her name may be. These so-called ladies ought to be made to give their own characters to the servants. A servant should have one day's rest in seven. The minister preaches in the pulpit to keep the Sabbath day holy. Let him see his on servants have a rest also on the Sabbath. A woman who has a home should not be made to sleep in. Let domestic service be made differ- ent, and then we will have no domestic problem to solve. I have a friend a servant who has to do whitewashing. If she refuses she has to leave. All girls should insist on a nine-hour day that go into service. A servant nowadays is like a machine. She must never be tired or ill, or if she is she gets the sack. In this Christian country it is a woman who is always down on a woman in domestic service. A servant is not looked up to like a mill girl. Trusting ladies who keep servants will read this letter and act upon it. MARY ANN.

June 6, 1914.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, 'JUNE 20, 1914.

GUARDIANS' NEW OFFICES.

Lowest Tender is Accepted.

BOARD AND PENSIONERS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding.

NEW OFFICES TENDERS.

Six tenders were,received for the building of the new offices, and that of Mr. Peter MaeLachlan for £7,49 was accepted. The other prices from firms, whose names were not disclosed, were: .0,145, £8,890, £8,805, £8,585, and A:9,100. Before the name of Mr. MacLachlan was dis- closed, Mr. Garnett asked: May we know what Mr. Owen . (the architect) anticipated? If I remember, the amount was below these tenders. Mr. Owen: The figure I gave was A:8,014. Mr. Garnett: I thought it was somewhere about £6,000.

Mr. McKinley: I thought it was • in the ~28,000'5. You made a very good guess, Mr. Owen. I move that No. 4 (Mr. MacLachlan's) tender he accepted. This was seconded. The Chairman: You know this is the lowest tender.

Mr. Brooks: We are not bound to accept this. The Chairman: Suppose we provisionally accept this, you can reject or otherwise when the name is disclosed. _ _

Mr. Brooks: Would it be well to go into corn- mittee?

The Chairman: The Board resolved itself into committee, and No. 4 (Mr. MacLachlan's) tender was accepted unanimously.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
DOCTORS AND THE POOR.

Guaranteed Payment Wanted.

- - - - ----- The Clerk reported that at a meeting of the Warrington Health Committee the previous night, Drs. Murray and Bennett, on behalf of the local branch of thelßritish Medical Asso- ciation, attended and pointed out the difficulties which had arisen in obtaining attendance of medical men at midwifery cases of persons in poor circumstances. They requested that the committee should guarantee the payment of doctors' fees, and stated that they would have a list prepared of doctors who would guarantee to attend.—The deputation were informed pat the Corporation had no power to go into such an arrangement and that it was a matter for the Board of Guardians, who paid the fees in all proper cases. The deputation were requested to communicate with that body. The Clerk said he had not heard anything from the deputation. On the proposition of Mr. Knowles the matter was allowed to lie on the table. . .

===== 0002601_19140620_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
OLD-AGE PENSIONS. Board Decline to Act on Resolution.

The Boa.rd were asked to support the following resolution: "That in view of the cost of living, rents, &c., especially in large towns and cities„ having increased by more than 15 per cent, since the Old Age Pensions Act came into force, representation be made to the Government with a view to securing a revision of the Act, by which the maximum (55.) now being paid be raised to 7s. 6d. per weet, and the age limit reduced to 65."

Mr. Knowles: Let it lie on the table; we are non-political.

. - - The Rev: A. 31. Mitchell: I propose we discuss the matter. Mr. Knowles: You do! Rev. A. 31. Mitchell: That is not a question of politics. The Chairman: It has been moved and seconded that the matter lie on the table. Rev. A. 31. Mitchell: I will move an amend- ment.

On a vote being taken, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell was the only member who was against the matter lying on the table.

DEPUTATION.

At a meeting of the Assessment Committee the Clerk and Mr. T'urness (chairman) were ap- pointed to attend a meeting of the Lancashire Union of Assessment Committees and Boards of Overseers appointed under Local Acts. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Are these meetings likely to be numerous?

Mr, McKinley: They pay their own expenses. :Laughter.) _

The Clerk : They have one in three years. Mr. Knowles: Do they walk there or go by train? (Laughter.)

NONCONFORMIST MINISTER.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell asked if. there had been anything done with reference to the ap- pointment of a Nonconformist minister at the Workhouse.

The Chairman: That matter is in progress. There is no report out yet.

SEVERE CASE OF BARBER'S ITCH.

62, Norbury Court-road, Norbury, London, S.W., England.—" About eight years ago the barber scraped my face rather badly and made it very sore. In a week or two, having had to be shaved every day, I found my cheeks and neck fairly smothered with small red spots. A couple of months showed that it had got very much worse, and I was told that I had a severe attack of barber's itch. After trying treatments for several months I finished up with them with a face like a badly cooked lobster. "After eighteen months of this I noticed the Cuticura Soap and Ointment at the chernist's and decided to try them. The effect of the treatment was really astounding for my face stopped irritating. In less than six weeks my face was as if it had never known the rash. That was six years ago and the trouble has never shown again." (Signed) R. Reynolds, Tan. 21, 1914. ===== 0002601_19140620_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
RIFLE LEAGTJE.

Ambitious Inauguration for

Warrington.

DETAILED SCHEME.

"To encourage friendly competition in rifle shooting" and other objects, it has been decided, at a recent meeting of the various miniature rifle clubs in the district, to form a league open to clubs within a radius of seven miles, the sub- scription for membership being ss. per annum. Mr. A. Shepherd presided at the meeting in the Whitecross Social and Recreation Club, and Mr. A. C. Binks read letters from the 4ollow- ing clubs: Appleton, Daresbury, Monks Hall, London and North-Western Railway, Rylands', Local Government Officers' Rifle Club and Whitecross. Subject to approval or alteration at the next meeting, the objects of the League are to en- courage friendly competition in this sport amongst surrounding clubs, and by means of competition to make members keen and increase the number of first-class shots in the district. ' The number of rifle clubs in the district (within the radius) is about twelve, although there are many works which can send out teams, but have not the benefit of a range. The en- deavour of the organisation of the League is also to encourage any who are ambitious to develop into first-class shots. Ladies will be 1 eligible to compete in League Competition. The members are most enthusiastic with re- gard to the League, and feel certain when the League is put on a permanent foundation, a good trophy will be offered for competition amongst the clubs, especially as the .League is open to a good radius, and so many encourage the development of shooting as a national asset. The League will open on handicap lines to provide greater interest to those who do not feel efficient to compete with clubs from " scratch.' The organising secretary, Mr. A. C. Binks, Whitecross Rifle Section, will be pleased to re- ceive notice from any club interested, give in- formation and extend to them an invitation to the public meeting of members of rifle sections in the district to be held early in July.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
A FEUDAL RELIC.

Eleventh Century Land System Useless. FAIR PLAY ALL ROUND

The Progressive Labour League's open-air campaign was • continued on Monday evening, when an exceedingly large Bridge Foot audi- ence was presided over by Mr. James Heywood. Mr. H. S. Seddon, of Manchester, who was the chief speaker; dealt in telling fashion with the Liberal party's land proposals.

LIBERALS AND LABOURITES. 'The CHAIRMAN said the workers of the country were determined that they were not going to be " sat upon" as they had been in the past. They thought it was only right that everyone who did a day's work should receive a fair day's pay. (Hear, hear.) The Liberal Government were giving to the labouring classes more than they had ever received before, but, of course, they could not say they were giving them more than they ought to do. It was always a consolation to those who had been oppressed to see that there were people endeavouring to remove those things which had ;been obstacles to prosperity and progress in the past. (Hear, hear.) w After giving a brief resume of Progressive legislation within the last few years, the Chair- man asked: "What do our Labour friends want?" If they followed tie papers week by week and heard the Labour speakers at different meetings, they were told that the Liberals were robbing the Labour party of their programme. If the Liberals were taking their programme and getting it passed into, law, why should t7hey complain? (Hear, hear.) He thought the Labour party ought to help the Liberals for- ward, instead of trying to put the clock back again in some instances. (Hear, hear, and ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19140620_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
_PROGRESSIVE LIBERALISM.

Mr. SEDDON said that whilst they had occa- sion to be grateful for all the beneficent measures which had been passed, they were all unanimous that there could be no Government, 'no State founded in a right and proper man- ner, which lived only in the record of its achievements. Therefore, if the Liberal Gov- ernment was to maintain the position which it had achieved it could only be by once having set their hands to the plough they went for- ward with their great work for the winning of liberty, peace and prosperity for all the people in the country. (Hear, hear.)

The Old Order Changeth. In what he described as a "calm and dis- passionate manner," the speaker then dealt with the Government's land proposals, which were being made in reference to the land. The day had passed when the land of the country was "parcelled out." They knew how it was given by the king to his feudal lords for services rendered and to be rendered. That old feudal system had gone for ever; but in this 20th cen- tury we had a land system which was a relic of those days. (Hear, hear.) We had a land system' which ,dated back to the 11th, 12th and 13th centuries." It was found at every point that the two things' did not harmonise; what was right in the 11th century was not possible of execution in the 20th century. l'hey had come to the conclusion that something would hare to be done, and that was necessary for the State to intervene. Liberals and Conservatives were both agreed on that, but what did the Con- servatives propose to do? Lord Lansdowne had stated what were undoubtedly the ConserVative land proposals, but he (the speaker) was of the opinion that it would be hard to find a scheme which worked more beneficially for the present landowners than that of the Tory Party. The Liberal party's proposals would have a benefi- cent effect, not only on the countryside, but in the towns. (Hear, hear.) The Root of the Evil.

In one month 12,000 people left one county in England and went abroad to seek their fortunes. There was a cry for labour and labourers in the country, and yet there was such an exodus. When they inquired the cause for this flow of the life blood of the country month by month they found the land system at the bottom of it all. They not to palter with effect, but to get right down to bed-rock truth, and there find the cause and a remedy for it. They found that at the root of all the trouble was the land system, to remedy which the Liberal Govern- ment had set out. They found that the agri- cultural labourer had nothing to which he could look forward but the cold grey walls of the workhouse, and he suggested that prospect was not such as would keep their young men and women in the country. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140620_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
The Labourer's Ladder.

That was where the Liberal party were going to begin with their proposals, and secure for the agricultural labourer a fair week's wage for a fair week's work, and give him the incentive to work on the land. They were going to secure fair play all round for the agricultural labourer. They were going to give him a sanitary cottage in which to dwell; the means whereby he could rise from the position in which he had been born, and, instead of spending the whole of his ' life as an agricultural labourer, rise up the ladder the Liberal party were going to place at his feet, until he got a small holding,. then a farm and afterwards a larger farm. The farmer would have security of tenure, and the landlord would receive a fair rent for his land. ' In the town the Liberal proposals would secure the abolition of slums—that festering canker present in all our towns and cities; security of tenure for the shopkeeper and com- pensation for improvements which he had brought about. Last, but by no means least, they would make it possible for councils and local authorities to secure land for public im- provements on better terms than they did at present. No fabulous prices would be paid for the land, but a fair and just price would be given for that which was needed for the benefit of the communities. Support the Government. 1 He urged them all to help the Government in their proposals by sending a Liberal member for Warrington at the next election. He be- lieved they would be grateful in the coming years for the help' they gave in the great land campaign hi securing freedom and better con- ditions for the people and greater opportunities for the children. (Applause.) Liberals appealed to the country with confidence and hoped that when the hour came they would give the pro- posals their sYmPathv and votes, and help for- ward the Liberal policy and the Liberal pro- gramme. (Applause.)

s, PINCHING" CARSON GUNS. On the proposition of Mr. SANDERS, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the speaker and chairman. After referring to the Tory tactics in regard to Borne Pule, the speaKer said that when the Nationalists " pinched " the Ulster- ites' guns from under their noses the latter did not turn on them and shoot, but went running round after Carson and F. E. Smith. (Laugh- ter.) That would be how all the talk of fight- ing frpm the Tories would end. Home Rule was now settled and the Plural Voting Bill would also he passed before the General Election, and " One man, one vote," would be the motto for all. (Applause.)

The Ways of the Household. For many years the bulk of the populace thought that the only substances that nourished the body were meat and bread: They were un- aware of the marvellous nutritive properties of grape sugar. When doctors were confronted with so many cases of suffering, brought about by the excessive use of meat, they began to edu- cate the public. They preached the doctrine of grape sugar, a substance that is all nutriment and easily assimilated. Little good could he done by talking of grape sugar, unless people were told where to find it. And so the great doctors urged everybody to eat Currants; not on occasion but every day, and more than once a day: Of course, it was something of a revelation to millions of good folk that a tiny s, and inexpensive fruit like the Currant was one of Nature's most powerful nutrients. Now they they have discovered that all the claims put for- ward on behalf of the Currant are perfectly true, the Currant ranks amongst the popular foods of the day. Here is one way of enjoying it. LUNCH CAKE.

a lb. Currants, 1 lb. flour, 6 ozs. dripping (or a mixture of lard, dripping and butter), 6 ozs. sugar, 2 ozs. candied peel, 1 egg, pint milk, teaspoonful carbonate of soda, flavouring. Method: Rub dripping into the flour, add sugar, Currants and peel, dissolve the soda in the milk (sour milk will do), add to the beaten egg, and make the cake mixture moderately soft. Bake in one tin 11 hours, or two tins about one hour.

alpirrii Orin.

The Sunday school anniversary services in connection with Ellesmere-street Wesleyan Methodist Chapel were held in Bold-street Church on Sunday, when the services were con- ducted by the Rev. W. WARRINGTON P.S.A.

Mr. W. Clegg presided over the meeting of the Warrington P.S.A., held on Sunday in the Park Picture Palace, when an inspiring address upon "Heaven" was given by Mr. Arthur Lumb. The P.S.A. Band, under the conductorship of Mr. J. E. Hannan, suitably played "Mystic Beauty," "The Shepherd's Dream," and otheil:.selections, whilst Mr. F. Tubb gave one of Mendelssohn's compositions upon the flute. The solos were rendered by Miss Hipkiss and Mr. Turton, the accompanists being Mrs. Massey, Miss Watts, and Mr. E. Hannan. ===== 0002601_19140620_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
_ SELF-CONTROL.

A stirring address was given on Sunday by Mr. Frank K. H. Davies, of the White Cross League, on "Self-control," at Holy Trinity Sunday School. Taking as his text the passage, "I keep under my body, and keep it under subjection" (St. Paul), he said self-control was necessary in young and old men and women. Any loss of self-control was followed by weak- ness and decay, bringing dishonour to them- selves and their own. Many young men starting out into life were told by their parents, "Make a name for yourself, and, above all, make money." Not a word was spoken of the perils and temptations which great cities hold out. As a result many a young man plunged to his fate, innocent and unwarned, and after a time became, if he had not an exceptionally strong mind, another of those babbling lunatics so often attributed to the drink craze.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

The Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday was presided over by Mr. Aspinall, Of Wigan. In the course of his remarks, the Chairman referred to the urgent necessity for unity of service; they must walk and work together for the advancement of Christ's kingdom. Mr. W. Street, who gave the address, spoke upon the life and work of Moses, taking for his subject "A Good Choice." The life of Moses, he said, was divided into three periods of forty years—with Pharaoh's daughter, with his rela- tives, and as the leader of the Israelites in their journeyings. Desiring rather to serve God amongst His people, Moses denied himself all the advantages of a ruler's house. The speaker concluded by asking the men to make a careful choice as to their mode of life, point- ing out that so much depended on a present good one. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ, Mr. Street suitably rendered " Mother, beg earnestly," and Mr. C. Cotton read the lesson.

FREE CHURCH COUNCIL. Service on the Fair Ground.

The first of a series of four open-air services to be held on the second Sunday in each month during the summer months, under the auspices of the Warrington Free Church Council, took place on Sunday evening at the Fair Ground. crowd assembled. The Revs. T. T. James, H. Councillor R. Henshall presided, and a large Ogden, W. A. Beckett and A. J. Chessum assisted in the ser- vice. The Rey. T. T. James delivered an in- spiring address, which he based upon Matthew xxvii. 22, "What shall I do, then, with Jesus, which is called Christ?" This, said the speaker, was a question which every man was bound to ask himself sooner or later, from which there could be no escape. Those who had read Beg- bie's "Broken Earthenware" knew that if they accepted Him, He would hold them secure from all evil.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
a COME TO CHURCH."

The Free Churches' Great Campaign.

The Free Churches are embarking upon a great campaign, their object being to secure the attendance at church of every man, woman and child in England and Wales on a Sunday in January next year. In order to accomplish this it is hoped that representatives of the Churches will visit every home throughout the country and leave a personal invitation for every in- mate to put in an appearance at church. , Thousands of visitors will be requisitioned in the accomplishment of this task, and there will be an army of superintendents and officers -to engineer this tremendous undertaking. It is expected that the simultaneous visitation will be arranged for an early date in January, and the Sundays that everybody will be asked to attend church are the 24th and 31st January. The "Come to Church" Campaign is not to be re- stricted to the Free Churches, and is not in any sense a proselytising agency. The National Council assure us that it is not being promoted in any denominational or sectarian sense, but with the wide sco appeal. 1 pe and urgency of the Gospel

QUEEN ALEXANDRA'S 70TH BIRTHDAY.

The King Proposes to celebrate December 1, when Queen Alexandra will attain the age of seventy, by a dinner, at which as 'many of her descendants and relatives as possible will be present. It is understood that the dinner will take place at Sandringham, and that the Ger- man Emperor and Empress will be present, to- gether with the Duke and Duchess of Cumber- land, the Duke and Duchess of Brunswick, and Prince and Princess Henry of Prussia. It is also hoped that the King and Queen of Den- mark, and the King and Queen of the Bellenes. together with the Crown Prince and Princess of Sweden, will be present.

The Complaint the Public have against Road Hogs.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
A RACING CURIOSITY.

I well remember the day when I had my first mount. It was an ordinary with solid tyres. I can never forget the feeling of pride that I had when I brought—not rode—my machine from the makers. In the light of developments that have taken place since those days I do not think, however, that I should like to enter into a com- petition against the safety. That the ordinary is not extinct is apparent to everyone, since a number of these machines are still to be seen on the roads. Only the other day I saw a veteran cyclist riding one of these curiosities down the Strand, and I could but marvel at the ease with which he controlled his machine and the dex- terity with which he wormed his way through the traffic. That such a cycle could be used at the present day for racing, and with a success- ful result, I never thought possible. Yet such is the case. tinder the auspices of the Elite Cycling Club a Mr. It. Somerset competed in a twenty-five mile race, being accorded twenty minutes start. His time was 1 hour 27 minutes 12 seconds, whereas the time of the fastest rider of a safety was 1 hour 14 minutes 27 seconds. With regard to the question of speed one won- ders what we have gained during the past fifteen years.

A Motor Road to Brighton. As a cyclist I have been interested in the news that a new roadway for motorists is proposed from London to Brighton. It is not for the reason that I shall be allowed to pedal my way along this road, but that the existing thorough- fare will then be rid of the dust-raising motor car. Ido not think that on the whole it can be said that we are against motor cars. We realise that they are a sign of the times, but we are against that section of the community who can only be described as road hogs. One has only to read the papers every day to come to the conclusion that this class of motorists is on the increase. Because one man has succeeded in evading capture, after causing the death of a cyclist, it appears that others imagine that they can do likewise. To-day the pleasures of cycling are almost counter-balanced by the dust,the glare of headlights and the risk from reckless drivers. If this road is constructed we may hope that others will follow; then we shall be able to enjoy to the full those experiences that we have had in the past. The Action of the C.T.C.

_ That special roads for motorists are becoming a necessity must be apparent to all who use the public thoroughfares. In the preceding para- graph we have referred to that class of motor- ist who causes damage and injury, and then, losing all sense of decency, makes off as rapidly as possible. The public mind is very short—we talk of a nine day's wonder—and we are apt to forget the things that happen only two or three weeks afterwards. Fortunately for us ag cyclists, the C.T.C. have decided to take this matter up, and they have offeted a reward of £5O for the discovery of the car responsible for the terrible, tragedy that occurred at Lee. I fully appre- ciate the fact that the majority of motorists and cyclists do not want a monetary reward for what they may do in this connection, but at the same time we cannot get over the fact that there are a number of motorists on the roads at the present time who have no thought for any- one but themselves. These are the people who will be responsible for the passing of severer laws regulating the traffic of motor driven vehicles on our roads.

Noisy Motor Cycles. The "Motor Cycle" has some pertinent re- marks on the question of the silence of motor cycles, and as they concern so many of my readers, I quote as follows: "We have now had time to consider the effects of the Local Govern- ment Board regulations concerning the effici- ency of silencers on motor cycles, and, though we should be the last to stir up the question again in view of the circumstances in which motor cyclists found themselves only a short time ago, we must confess that the Local Gov- ernment Board orders have not met with the success they deserve. It is unfortunate that a certain section of motor cyclists have not been wise or considerate enough to take the Local Government Board's timely hint, and ensure that their machines conform to the law, but the fact remains that one out of every twenty motor cycles to be seen about our roads even at this period is decidedly on the noisy side."

Noise Not Necessary. It is not as though it were necessary to have a noise at the present time with our motor cycles, for this is far from the case, otherwise we might hesitate a long time before drawing attention to the matter, but that there is noth- ing to be gained from the use of a noisy machine has been proved on many different occasions. We are, therefore, 'moved to make another ap- peal to this inconsiderate section to pay a little more regard to law and order. We do this in the general interests of motor cyclists, knowing, as we do, that if riders continue to career about the country creating unnecessary noise, it will merely mean that further legislation will only be a question of time, and that the vigilance of the police will be increased.

CO-OPERATORS AND TRADE UNIONISTS.

What a Fusion Would Mean. In an interview with our Newcastle corre- spondent, says the "Manchester Guardian," Mr. John Hill, the general secretary of the Boilermaker's Society, said with regard to the proposed combination of co-operators and trade union'tsts that the national committees on both sides looked upon an amalgamation as certain. It was a very much greater thing, he said, than the recent amalgamation of trade unims, for nine or ten millions of people would be directly affected. The scheme meant the trade unions investing their funds with the Co-operative Societies and doing all their banking, including the transfer- ring of funds from trade union head offices and branches, through the Co-operative Wholesale Society bankers. When trade disputes occurred co-operators would finance trade unions when the latter's own funds were exhausted.

WHY LAMP TIMES VARY.

Every club card that has been forwarded to me this year differs in the secretary's estimate of lamp time. The times given vary as much as 27 minutes. But why do the Sankey Bridges cyclists have to light lamps at 9.18, whilst the Warrington C.C. can save their combustibles till 9.44? And, in a whisper. I would advise the latter club to provide some light or other before 9 o'clock on the 29th of August, or there will be trouble, unless they intend to walk home from the Angel at Knutsford. The fact is that at midsummer sunset at War- rington occurs 24 minutes after the same phe- nomenon at Greenwich. I gave the lamp time

for last Saturday as 9.35, and standing on the Canal Bridge at Acton at 8.30 we watched the sun just preparing to set. Greenwich lamp time was then 9.14. - The reason for this is two-fold. First, as we are 21- degrees to the west, the sun passes over Wv rrington 10 minutes later than over Green- wich. The other 14 are accounted for by the fact that we are 2 degrees to the north. This is more difficult to explain, but it can be real- ised by remembering that as we go northwards sunset occurs later and later, until in the north ' of Europe, within the Arctic circle, the sun has not gone down at 12 p.m.; that is, the midnight sun can be seen, as at the North Cape. The precise lamp time for any place in the British Isles may be found by reference to the C.T.C. hand book.

1 his once great club did the pastime the ser- v;cc of settling once for all in the Appeal Court the ridiculous claim of the police to insist on Greenwich sunset applying to the whole of Great Britain. It was abOut 1904 that a case at Bris- tol was carried up to the higher court, and when it was pointed out that under such a law, cyclists at Wick, for instance, would be coin- pelled to light up whilst the sun was still shin- ing, the judge at once declared for the cyclists, and said the case ought never to have been brought up.

A MIDSUMMER AURORA.

I shall never forget the Ist July, 1908. A party of us had been to Arley, and stayed until after lamp time, as it was Walking Day holidays. We rode home, however, without lamps, arriv- ing about 10.30. Then, after writing a letter, I cycled to the G.P.O. and back, in clear day- light, at 11.30, everyone wondering what was the reason that night would not come on. Next morning, however, the news came that a most brilliant aurora had been observed in Norway, and had occasioned the long spell of daylight enjoyed by us in England.

SPORTS WITH ST. GEORGE'S.

Our destination on Saturday was Halebank, the outward journey being made through ram- worth, Appleton and Ditton. Turning under the bridge at Halebank Station and up Finch- lane, we reached Finch Cottage, where an excel- lent tea was served by Mrs. Fleetwood—and we can strongly recommend this place. The ride in the broiling sun seemed to evaporate the energy of most of the members, and resting was pre- ferred to scenery viewing. Some, however, in- dulged in a tug-of-war—ladies v. gents—but the result was rather doubtful, the rope breaking at a critical moment. The return was made through Halewood, taking the turn at the Brick- wall Hotel, and on through Hough Green and Farnworth. Although this part of the county is not famed for its beauty, nevertheless it makes an enjoyable ride.

RIDES WITH AN OBJECT.-5.

Perhaps no village near Warrington is so well provided with means of access as Kelsall, on the Manchester—Chester-road. I know at least eight direct roads, most of them, oh, happy cyclist, inaccessible to all except the most ad- venturous motorists.

A delightful route is from Hatchmere corner along the switchback to the first farm under the railway bridge. There turn left round the duck pond and up Brine's Brow. Keep to the right after half a mile, and to the left at the next turning, and the middle of Kelsall village is soon reached. A still more pleasant way is Co leave the switchback about the middle. The road to the left through the heart of the forest soon takes you to Eddisbury Lodge Farm, where tea may be taken. The turn to the right takes us past Forest Farm into the road mentioned above. Ashton Haws, the magnificent park on the right-hand, belongs to Mr. Boger Parr, and the whole lane is beautiful. A splendid view of Chester can be obtained through the trees, at the highest point of the road.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JUNE 20, 1914.

MR. ARTHUR Cl

tOSFIELD.

Mr. ARTHUR CROSFIELD has an extra- ordinary facility for getting directly at the heart of a subject. Noth- ing was better demonstrated, during the fine campaign for land reform which he recently headed in Warring- ton, than the prospective Liberal can- didate's natural gifts of concise expres- sion which experience is richly supple- menting. The campaign made a deep impression, and the evidence of its influence upon the minds of the party is being felt more as time passes. There was something in what Mr. CROSFIELH and his fine body of land campaigners had to say that was cal- culated to attract and hold the attention and it is only the expected that has hap- pened when men and women are found taking an enthusiastic interest in the Progressive programme of the near future.

For much of this Mr. CROSFIELD must take some personal credit. Discarding vulgarities as an aid of which he does not stand in need, Mr. CROSFIELD dealt with the proposals of his party to fur- ther better the conditions of his fellows in convincing and statesmanlike style. There is no call for extraneous aids when one can approach great subjects, as Mr. CROSFIELD approached them almost nightly for a fortnight with freshness of thought, energy of expres- sion, and earnestness of purpose, and impress his hearers with the soundness of his logic and the unanswerable nature of his case. We have known Mr. CROSFIELD for the whole of his life; for the greater part of twenty years he has been the Progressive standard- bearer for Warrington, and his friends and supporters are delighted to see that he is in such fine trim, in view of the contest that now lies immediately ahead. A candidate with a definite , policy, who can propound that policy with :moderation, yet with force, is a candidate who can be trusted to carry the standard he bears to success.

AN AWFUL DISCOVERY.

Silently, imperceptibly, unofficially, and altogether unauthorised, but very effectively, a National Volunteer Army has arisen in Ireland to counter-balance Sir EDWARD CARSON'S " Army " ; and this week LORD ROBERT CECIL sub- mitted a motion in the Commons, "to call attention to the growing danger caused by the existence of Volunteer forces in Ireland." For two and a half years the Ulster Tories have been organising and arming to overawe Par- liament, and resist a law which they had every reason to believe that Par- liament would pass. Instead of being disturbed at these preparations, the Tory party, which supported LORD ROBERT CECIL'S motion with absolute unanimity, did everything possible to encourage it. Gun-running, the coer- cion and commandeering of public ser- vices in Ulster, were praiseworthy acts, because they were carried out by an organisation which is in league with the Tory party. Now that the force which is drilling to resist the Home Rule Bill is faced with another force organised to support the aspirations of the great majority of the Irish people, LORD ROBERT CECIL and his party are deeply concerned at the supineness of the Government. When it is recalled that a short time ago elementary pre- cautions against lawless acts that there was reason to apprehend in Ulster, were denounced by the Opposition as a diabolical plot to provoke bloodshed, the colossal impudence of LORD ROBERT CECIL'S motion can be realised. It is acknowledged that the second Army neutralises the first Army—that is the trouble ; and politicians, starting from a new and fairer mark,may find the solution of the Irish problem without any further intervention of the Tory threat of "civil war."
