PASSING WEEK.

THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

The conference between the representatives of the Education Committee and the Board of Education has taken Place. Our readers are familiar with the stand the local authority have taken, and will be prepared to learn that the London authorities are to be told in plain terms what we propose to do and not to do. The School Management Committee will ask the Education Committee on Monday night to inform the Board that the proposal of the Governors to provide a school for 200 boys is too ambitious; that the financial proposals require further consideration; that the Educa- tion Committee cannot give any guarantee to continue to be responsible for any deficit in the school accounts up to £4OO a year, because of the immediate prospect of the committee being called upon to provide a Secondary School for Girls, leaving the present Secondary School available for boys only. Finally, says the reso- lution, the guarantee to meet the deficiency "will only be considered on the basis of the municipalisation of the Grammar School." Another minute shows that the deficiency to be met in last year's Grammar School accounts was not £4OO, but only £29 9s. id.

OUT-DOOR RELIEF MAY "PAY." In-the parliamentary debate on Local Govern- ment, Lord Cavendish-Bentinck and Mr. Toul- mM made some pertinent criticism on out-door relief. In his reply, Mr. Herbert Samuel made an interesting pronouncement. He stated that a circular would shortly be issued to the Guar- dians on the subject of out-relief to widows and children. "It would lay stress on the neces- sity of maintaining the unity of the family, and not requiring the separation of children from the mother, and also on the need of the relief being adequate to the purpose." Speaking from knowledge, we may say that there is room for considerable improvement in our own local administration. No doubt elected representatives sometimes have an idea that they serve the ratepayers best by devoting their energies to cutting down the amount of out- relief. But such a policy often -proves to be singularly short-sighted. If once out-relief drops below the proper level of subsistence, health—the chief asset of the working classes— is undermined. The burden of ill-health among the poor falls finally upon the community. Pauperism will never be eradicated by inade- quate out-relief, which .is in reality a most extravagant policy for the ratepayer..

A GOOD PIIILIP CARPENTER STORY. A correspondent, " J.N.," sends us the follow- ing capital story: The account of Philip Car- penter, which Mr. Arthur Mounfield has written for "The Examiner," brought back very fresh to my memory a story which set all Warrirfgton laughing. When taking his usual early morn- ing swim in the canal, he was horrified one morning to see a female figure sitting upon his clothes. He was ready to leave the water and made various splashes and noises to call atten- tion to his presence. But the lady had evidently come to enjoy the morning air and read very contentedly from a book. There were no other bathers and no help was forthcoming from any quarter. Philip was a bachelor, and a rather shy man with ladies at the best of times. Thi water was cold, and he had had much more than enough before the lady very calmly and with apparent absent-mindedness got up and walked away. When by a great effort Philip had got warmth into his limbs and was preparing for home, another shock awaited him. The supposed lady was one of his friends masquerading in a woman's gown and bonnet, and a shout of laughter from others who had been in hiding told the chief of the Swimming Academy that he had been the victim of a plot against his happiness. The story passed round the town, and the Philistines made the most of it. Philip himself was too good tempered to be angry, and after awhile began to enjoy the story himself.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
TEACIIING PROFESSION FOR GIRLS.

There can be little doubt that the teaching profession still has its attractions for girls, as Canon Morley Stevenson pointed out at the annual prize distribution at the Warrington Training College. There are counter-attrac- tions, it is true, for the educated woman—the ICivil Service and the professions which are lopening their doors to women, somewhat timidly, or, at all events, on a limited scale; but there are few callings that have such an accumulation of attractions as that of a teacher. To take the material advantages first. There is a commencing salary of £7O or £75 a year, rising to £l2O, and to more should a headship fortunately present itself. At sixty, or sixty- five, the teacher becomes entitled to a pension of £5O a year. Our reporters say that the young ladies indulged in laughter when the Principal mentioned this fact. Why they should have done so we do not understand. Was it that in their pride of youth and beauty, reference to the time when they will be sixty- five is a humorous matter? Yet they will be sixty-five some day, and it is not every woman who can say she will have I 1 a week at the time she will be approaching the period when she will be alinost "sans everything." Or was it the amount that seemed absurd beside the substantial sums to which they will become entitled as certificated mistresses? The living wage—much more than the average male trade unionist receives ; and the pension, which trade unionists are denied till they reach the age of seventy—did not exhaust Canon Steven- son's bright side of the picture. Teachers have holidays that run into months during the year. Again, a College training ' provides an education, the possession of which is a sure protection against any reverse of fortune or trouble except ill-health. But there is a greater allurement, that of being able to render the finest service that can be rendered the State by educating the citizens of the State, and so giving it the only foundation upon which it ,Than, with any assurance of success, continue I to build.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 20, 1914.

MY LETTERS.

The Queer Assortment That the Postman Delivers.

POLITICIANS AND MONEY LENDERS

Outlets for Display of Obsessed Class-Prejudice.

I do not know if the batch of letters the post- man delivers at my door is similar to that of others, but I do get a queer assortment. Naturally, of late, the amount of anti-Home Rule literature received has been great. From time to time I get political information from the National Constitution Defence Movement, of which Stafford Northcote is the secretary. It informs me that "the country is faced by the gravest crisis it has known for 250 years." Apart from Home Rule, other terrible dangers threaten us in 1914. In the labour world the spirit of revolution is abroad—Larkinism, Syn- dicalism, even the organised destruction of private property is advocated by the wild agita- tors who are fomenting Discord, Hate, and Anarchy, in this erstwhile peaceful and con- tented country." These statements would have a disturbing effect on the nerves of a peaceful citizen of "this erstwhile contented country" were it not for the mental reaction such extravagance creates. One wonders if the writers really believe what they so solemnly, affirm. Have they become so ob- sessed by class prejudice that they have lost their power of seeing things as they are? There is nothing new about this wild talk. Almost identical terms have again and again been used when any legislation has been proposed limit- ing monopoly rights and diminishing, the power of class ascendancy. Similar things were said at the passing of the Reform Act, the Abolition of the Corn Laws, Disestablishment of the Irish Church, and the like.

After these grave statements, and a good deal more of the same sort, we read "May we ask you as a supporter of Constitu- tional principles to contribute to our fighting fund? Every amount is welcome and will help to defeat Home Rule, Syndicalism, and Radical Land Robbery, and so save the country from Civil War and Revolution. We want everyone to help. Cheques, money orders, large sums, small sums, all are welcome, and all will go to- wards driving from place and power the worst and most corrupt Government of the last 100 years."

I fancy every Liberal Government has been so called for the last hundred years. I am told that "in Belfast many of the strong- est arguments against Home Rule are static; incapable of logical articulation; they are born of temperament, religion and tradition." What a pity arguments incapable of logical articulation are not convincing.

Bringing in the King. "King George has told us that England must wake up, and the one sign of the acceptance of this Royal command of wakefulness will be in our readiness to • resist to the uttermost a gigantic conspiracy to break up the Empire." How skilfully the King is brought in.

To Quench My Thirst. Some people seem to think that my consump- tion of liquors is enormous, and therefore well worth catering for. One man sends me a booklet which has on the front page a beautiful picture of the vineclad hills through which the Moselle flows. He tells me, "On comparison with other reliable wine lists, it will be found that my prices, consistent with the superior quality of my wines, are at least 40 per cent. below those of any other exporter." My special attention is called to the wines of 1911, "a year which is recognised as a great success, being full-flavoured and with natural sweetness."

All classes of whiskies and wines are recom- mended for ray use. "Old Highland" is soft and mellow, and none cheaper. "The Abbot" is mature, of extremely nice flavour and ab- surdly cheap. One drink is a nice flavour, a second t. fine flavour, a third a nice soft flavour, and so on as follows: A delicious flavour, very fine flavour, excellent flavour, nice mature flavour, beautiful soft flavour, good body and flavour, unique flavour, beautiful dry flavour, beautiful nutty flavour, fine mature flavour, and SO on until all appropriate adjectives are thread- bare.

To Dodge the Chancellor. I got a pamphlet the other day, which ap- peared to me in its early contents to promise a, method by which I could escape my financial obligations to the Chancellor of the Exchequer. I began it with zest; it turned out to be the old story of insuring my life. Money Lenders.

outward a _ ___....... I get many letters from people who seem very anxious to lend me money. These have all the all want to be of service to me, and say that if appearance of personal letters. They in immediate need of money—this is my chronic condition—from ..t5O to ,t 1,000,000 they would be willing to lend on my "note of hand and without security" any sum within these limits. The terms for these advances are most reason- able, all transactions are secret, liberality and simPlicity are the characteristics of their method.

Apparently my name stands so high that no inquiry would be made, and the crisp bank notes would come by return of post. What could I not do with this million?

Second-hand Jewels. A London firm wants to send at their own risk and expense for my inspection an assort- ment of second-hand jewels they have bought at wonderfully low prices. How full the world is of kind people, but as I do not care twopence for jewels the kindness in ray case is misplaced. To Fight Socialism.

This week I go-t-a.- serious-looking document in a blue envelope marked "Personal," and with blue lines upon it, indicating that the sender, te make sure of my getting it, had registered the package. My first thought was that the Chan= cellor of the Exchequer wanted something more out of me, or that it was a summons for some wrong I had never done. I opened the envelope with some anxiety. On the top of the enclosure a coronet was stamped. What did some noble lord want of me? The first few words made this clear. "We appeal to you as a business man to help us in the fight against the spread of Socialism, and Socialistic legislation." It proved to be a request that I should put the appeal before my co-directors and send money to Lord Abinger, as treasurer of the Anti- Socialist Union of Great Britain. The noble lord felt sure if I gave the matter my serious consideration I would come to the conclusion that this union was working for the prosperity of the country, and in the interests of the large employers of labour. It would be interesting to see the names of the large employers who con- tribute, but there is little chance of this, as we are told " the names of the subscribers are never divulged." I wonder why, if the object is the prosperity of the country? The funds are to be used for opposing Socialist candidates for seats in the House of Commons and in borough elections. Now, while I have no leanings to Socialism, as generally understood, I have a great respect for every earnest man who, accord- ing to his light, works for the betterment of his country and his kind. And this generally applies to Socialists. In public bodies Socialists make far better representatives and are more needed than the type of man who appears so obsessed with the necessity of safe-guarding pro- perty that he does not realise the importance of safe-guarding, what is a far more important thing, human life. This document, like all the others, finds its home in the waste-paper basket. C. E. PARKER.

OUR POPULAR MAYOR.

"At Home " to Friendly

Societies.

BROTHERLY APPRECIATION.

"For the first time in the history of Warring- ton, friendly society brothers and sisters have been invited to attend at the Town Hall to re- ceive the hospitality of the Mayor and Mayoress." There is a world of meaning behind this statement, made on the steps of the Town Hall on Thursday evening. It means, in the first plate, that every member of the Friendly i Society movement n the town can take the hand of the chief magistrate and his wife and call them "brother" and "sister." This bond of sympathy between the Mayor and the town's people is bound to have a beneficial effect on the good government of the municipality. Those for whom the Mayor has worked so assiduously are not prone to recognise what they owe to so worthy a member, who has brought such high honour on the movement, and, as an outward and visible sign of their esteem and affection, they presented the Mayor and Mayoress with a handsome solid silver tea and coffee service, the tray bearing the inscription : Presented to Councillor Bro. Peter Peacock and Sister M. Peacock by the Friendly Societies of Warrington, in celebration of the election of Bro. Peter Peacock to the• Mayo- ralty of the County Borough of Warrington, 18th June, 1914."

There was an exceedingly large attendance at the "At Home" which the Mayor gave at the Town Hall on Thursday, and the unanimity with which all endorsed the sentiments ex- pressed, proved his Worship's popularity with the people. Prior to the official reception, P.P.G.M. T. P. BRADSHAW presented the Mayoress—who wore a charming dress of white embroidered muslin with a beautiful bouquet of roses, lilies of the valley, and smilax, on behalf of the societies. The guests were received in the Town Hall, which had been tastefully decorated with plants and flowers, and the people then proceeded on to the lawn, the park being reserved entirely for those invited. Mr. T. Boothroyd's orchestra and Crosfield's Band discoursed delightful music whilst the guests partook of the delicacies which were served alfresco in such an excellent man- ner, according to the admirable arrangements made by Mr. and Mrs. Mitchell.

THE BOND OF SYMPATHY. ,In making the presentation, Grand Master EDWIN ALLEN, of the Order of Druids, said that was a unique gathering, in that that was the first time in Warrington brethren and their wives had been invited to the Town Hall to receive the hospitality of the Mayor and Mayor- ess. The gathering ivae large and representa- tive of all the Friendly Societies in the town. Friendly Societies owed their inception to sym- pathy, which was the outcome of a desire to assist those who were in need. They often heard that the old sentiments of their fore- fathers were dead. Whilst not accepting that statement as a fact, he did say that if any- thing had assisted in burying those sentiments it was the Insurance Act. He would not com- plain about the birth of that Act—he thought that Friendly Society members welcomed it in principle—but they did complain about its machinery and the cumbrous regulations which accompanied it.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Proud of their Mayor.

Turning to the Mayor, he said': We are aware of the great financial sacrifices you made when you accepted the mayoralty of this borough, and we are proud to think those sacrifices were not made to obtain personal aggrandisement, but rather that you might assist and honour those of whom this gathering is so representative. You have a long and honourable membership of the Ancient Order of Foresters, and we have all watched your career with pleasure ard pride. Friendly Societies have need to be thankful for those who have joined them such as yourself, not for obtaining as much out of them as they can possibly get, but for the purpose of helping them to develop, and giving them as Much bene- fit as they possibly can. (Hear, hear.) I have now very great pleasure in presenting you, Bro. Peacock, and you, Sister Peacock, on behalf of the united Friendly Societies in Warrington, with this beautiful solid silver tea and coffee service. I have been asso'ciated with many pre- sentations, but I have never been associated with one which was so ungrudgingly contri- buted to and so splendidly assisted. We trust you will always look upon this present with pride and pleasure. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140620_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S REPLY.

_ A " Scandal " to the Town. The 'MAYOR said they had made the best arrangements-possible, but they could not help the threatening state of the weather—a slight shower having just terminated—and he hoped they would take the will for the deed. (Ap- plause.) They would look upon the present with the greatest possible pride. The old ideas of secrecy had been maintained throughout the arrangements for the presentation, for they did not know about it until all the money was col- lected, and they were asked to make a choice. There had been so many members from various Orders that he did not know which shake of the hand to give, so that if he had "rung the changes" he hoped they would forgive him. (Laughter.) He congratulated the committee , on the excellent arrangements they had made, and he also congratulated Bro. Allen on the fact that he had been elected to th-. 4 highest office in the Druids—the Grand Master of all England and Wales. (Hear, hear.) He also congratulated the Druids on the fact that even if Warrington were not a seaside or an attrac- tive place, they had arranged to hold their conference in the borough next year. He did not know who would be Mayor next year—'(A Voice: "We do ")—but he was sure whoever it was would give them a hearty welcome to the town, and that other societies would hold simi- lar gatherings in the town. He was- sorry he had to apologise for the inadequate accommoda- tion for a reception of that kind. He thought it was a scandal that for an industrial town like Warrington and with such a large population, it was impossible to get a building large enough to accotimodate such a large assembly as they , had that night. There was a good time coming when the people in Warrington would realise that they ought to have a place where they could accommodate large numbers of people, and house their officials in a proper manner, to the betterment of the whole town. He thanked them for attending in such large numbers, and for the many past favours they had bestowed upon him in his public life. He owed every- thing to Friendly Societies, and he thanked them for the practical sympathy they had handed out to him on many occasions, without which he would not have risen to that high and important position in the town. Ho thanked them for their words of encouragement, and he hoped both he and the Mayoress would live long to look on that present, and then hand it down to their family as an heirloom. (Applause.)

YOUTHFUL ATHLETES.

Mayor and Mayoress Present Trophies at St. James' School.

A PROUD RECORD.

A remarkable blending of success in the classroom and in the playing fields is provided in the annals of St. James' School, where, on Tuesday afternoon, the Mayor and Mayoress (Councillor Peter and Mrs. Peacock) attended to hand over to the school the " Dodds'" Asso- ciation Football Challenge Shield and medals. The school has also been successful in the Northern Union game, for the medals offered by the Warrington Club have gone to the St. James' scholars; whilst, further, in the team which gained the honour of ranking as the schoolboy champions of South-West Lancashire, this particular school had six representatives. The Head Master, Mr. W. H. Stansfield, pre- sided, and amongst those present in addition to the Mayor and Mayoress, were: The Rev. H. B. Firth (vicar), Rev. H. H. Corsbie (curate), Mrs. Stansfield, the teaching staff, and a few parents and friends of the children.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
ATHLETIC SUCCESSES.

- - - --- Mr. Stansfield said he was extremely pleased to welcome the Mayor and Mayoress, because no previous occupants of the position had shown such a regard for the children of Warrington. (Cheers.) The Schools Association Football League had been in existence for nine years. St. James' was one of the original schools which were represented in the competition, and was one of the only three schools which had been identified with the League during the whole of its existence, although that was the first occa- sion on which they had won that particular trophy. They had been more successful with their Rugby team, for they had only lost one match since the Schools Rugby League was formed in 1911. They were the champions in 1912, 1913, and also for the present year—(cheers) —but although every effort had been made to obtain those medals for presentation that day, they had been unsuccessful. Six of their boys were chosen to form part of the Warrington team which won the South-West Lancashire Cup, and probably because of that fact, they were given the privilege of holding that trophy for the first six weeks.

It was rather singular that eight of the boys were members of both winning teams in the Association and Rugby competitions. These boys were Newall, who was the captain of the two teams, Lockhart, Dunbobbin, Skelhorn, Smith, Plumb, Morrison and Munn. The girls also had had their successes, for in 1912 they won the rose bowl for rounders, and he had hopes it would come back again to its resting- place—(laughter)—this year. (Hear, hear.) All the successes in games, and he was very proud of them, could not have been accomplished with- out the enthusiastic work and co-operation of the teachers, amongst whom he mentioned Miss Turnbull, and Messrs. A. H. Cope, C. George and E. J. Mee. (Applause.) A record such as they held could not have been obtained with- out the hearty co-operation of a highly qualified, capable and loyal staff, of which no one was more proud than himself.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
Classroom Successes.

They also claimed successes in scholarships. It was pleasing to note that since 1908 they had won five Boteler Grammar School scholarships, and during the time the Trafalgar Essay prizes had been offered by Alderman Bennett, they had been suecessful for five years in gaining a prize, ivhilst on three occasions they held the shield as well. Further, since 1906, sixteen of the scholars had gained free admission to the Muni- cipal Secondary School, so that it would be seen they did not neglect .the educational part for the athletic.

Withdrawal of the Brightest Scholars. There was, however, ..pne drawback from the school point of view, to such educational suc- cesses, and that was that the very sharpest and cleverest of the pupils, who would continue to bring credit to the school and the teacher, were withdrawn just at the tine when their talents would most conspicuously attract flip., attention of H.M. Inspectors. He doubted very niiich if sufficient allowance was made for the absence of the brightest of their pupils when the school's work was assessed. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

THE PRESENTATIONS. To the accompaniment of loud cheers, the Mayor presented the " Dodds' " Shield to the captain, Newell, and the Mayoress handed the medals to the following: Harry Newhll, Arthur Lawton, George Dunbobin, Frank Plumb, Harry Smith, David Morrison, John Munn, Arthur Green, William Skelhorn, George Lockhart, George Dickenson, Arthur Bradshaw and Ed- ward Beddall.

The members of the Rugby team, whose medals had not arrived, are: H. Newall (cap- tain), W. Skelhorn, P. Plumb, A. Armitage, R. Bibby, G. Lockhart, J. L. Munn, A. Mather, A. Tones, G. Dunbobbin, D. Morrison, A. Mather and C. Brimelow.

The Rev. H. B. Firth proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor and Mayoress. The scholars at that school were receiving a thor- ough education, and the Mayor was doing a good deal to promote the sporting instinct. They must not forget, however, that there was the spiritual side of their work, and he was glad to say the children there knew so much about the Bible. (Applause.)

The Rev. H. H. Corsbie seconded, and the carrying of the motioji was signalised by loud cheering.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S SUGGESTION.

The Mayor, in his reply, spoke of the pleasure it gave both the Mayoress and himself to be present. The first thing that drew his atten- tion, as he entered the room, was the excep- tionally good "Roll of Honour" oirrhe black- boards at the back of the room, and he made the suggestion that it would be an incentive to the scholars if a permanent record could be ar- ranged. (Applause.) If that was done, there would be keen competition amongst the children to have their names put up. (Hear, hear.) The teachers deserved the greatest thanks, for such work could not have been performed but for their enthusiasm. (Applause.) An admirable programme was rendered by the children, which included: Part song,' "I Know a Dank," by the upper girls; song, "The Cuckoo," upper boys; part song, "The Good Greenwoods," upper boys; recitation, "Vita Lampada," W. Lowe; part song, upper girls; solo, "Long. Long Ago," Skelhorn; part song, "Sweet and Low," upper girls; and round, upper boys. During the proceedings Florrie Reynolds, one of the scholars, presetted a bouquet on behalf of the school, to the Mayoress.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
BEANIONT SCHOOL SUCCESSES.

Yesterday afternoon Mr. J. Moore Murray, Director of Education, visited the Beamont Council Schools and presented Annie Houghton Baines, the champion girl in the recent athletic festival, with a beautiful gold pendant and chain, the gift of Mr. W. H. Wallington. Mr. Murray also presented the Coronation Shield to the boys, and delivered an address upon "Sport," urging the boys to put forth all the best in them, not for gain, but happiness; to avoid gambling, and not to mind if they were often called back to the starting-point. If they did their best they would never regret it. (Ap- plause.) Johnson, one of the boys, then presented Mr. Murray with a framed photograph of the suc- cessful group. Mr. D. Morgan Tames moved a vote of appre- ciation of Mr. Murray's visit and for his sound advice about "playing the game." In second- ing, Mr. W. Mason, headmaster, said that he had been proud of the boys as he watched them at the sports. Warrington was fortunate in having a true sportsman as adviser to its Edu- cation Committee, (Applause.) Cheers were given for Mr. Murray and the school, and appropriate songs were rendered.

STO RY OF HILL CLIFFE.

Mc. G. A. Dunlop Discusses Some Vague Points

WITH ADULT SCHOOL RAMBLERS.

Beautiful weather favoured tbe second ramble of the season arranged under the auspices of the Warrington Adult Schools for last Saturday afternoon. The fairly large party met in Vic- toria-square, Stockton Heath, and sauntered to Hill Cliffe burial ground, where the leader, Mr. G. A. Dunlop, of the Warrington Museum, gave in interesting fashion, the result of his study of the history, authentic and unauthentic, of the church and burial ground. Mr. Dunlop, instead of mixing up all the sec- tions of the subject, took them in three natural self-containing divisions, and dealt with all the established facts as apart from theories under each of three heads—Baptist congregations, the Baptist burial ground, and the Baptist Chapel.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
BAPTIST CONGREGATIONS.

There had been, he said, Baptist congregations in this country from the earliest time, and the the doctrine of immersion prevailed. About the year 400 infant sprinkling was introduced, and was fostered by the Church of Rome with such ardour that in the year 600 2,100 Christians were massacred by Austin of Rome for the simple offence of being Baptists. The early congrega- tion, in days before the existence of chapels, probably met in the houses of the neighbour- hood. There was evidence that many local families were Baptists—it was recorded that Roger Holland was put to death as a Baptist, June 27th, 1558, the year Queen Mary— " Bloody Mary "—died. Mary had her spies, usually officers in the army, scattered over all the land, and an adverse report of the spy on religious matters often meant death. In 1800 a cemented baptistry was discovered on the site of Hill Cliffe Chapel, and a hole, hewn in the solid rock, supposed to have served the purpose of a hiding-place from persecutors, had also been found. From 1792 to recent times the chief places of baptism were at the brook at Lower Walton and at Cann-lane, Appleton. It was known from such a definite source as a legal deed of the Warburton family that the first [known] minister was John Warburton, second son of Sir John Warburton, of Arley, who lived at Broomfields and died in 1594. He was succeeded by Mr. Daintith (whose farm was visited later in the afternoon). In 1642 Thomas Tillam was minister; his wife was a native of the district. A Bible of the period (1638) still exists.

The Puritan Impetus. The congregation began to grow in 1652, the direct effect of the influence of Cromwell's suc- cessful Army. Many of the Protector's officers were Baptists, and they took an active part in forming churches, especially in garrison towns. The well-known Colonel John Wigan founded the first Baptist Church in Manchester; and, as the last great battle of the civil wars took place near Warrington in 1648, there was a strong suggestion that the Baptists of the victorious army sowed the seeds of the church here.

ANTIQUITY OF THE BURIAL GROUND. There was a tradition of a Lollard burial ground at Hill Cliffe in the 14th century. That might have been so. It was alleged that the ground contained stones bearing the dates 1357, 1414, 1522 and 1599, and writers had been found to uphold the theory on such flimsy evidence. The late Mr. William Beamont, who had done a great work in local history and required actual proof for all he said and believed, rejected this theory, not only urging that Arabic numerals were not in use in this country in the 14th cen- tury, and that the type of tombstone on which the dates appeared had not come into use, but he undertook to 'produce the forgers who had altered dates on stones in the burial ground. The authentic date of the formation of the graveyard was given in a deed dated 1663, which was granted to Thomas Millington, of Appleton, gentleman, and William Rowlinson, of But- tonwood, yeoman; the names on the deed being John Wigan (the Colonel John Wigan, of Crcim- well's Army, who established the church at Manchester), Lydia Morris, John Ileawood, John Wigan, jun., and Henry Cockson. Further authentic evidence as to the begin- nings of the burial ground was found, accord- ing to Mr. Arthur Mounfield, in the diary of Matthew Henry, who records the fact that he buried Hannah Aubrey there in 1709, adding that her father was the first to be buried in that ground. The earliest dates on authentic stones are:— Maria Heslop, 1664. Joshua Seddon, 1672. Elizabeth S Eddon, the wife of Joshua ,Seddo n, 1679.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE CHAPEL WAS. BUILT.,

There was no evidence of a building at Hill Cliffe prior to 1688. A chapel built after the Revolution had mullioned windows. The meet- ing-house in Roebuck-yard was said to be older than that at Hill Cliffe. The tradition that Cromwell worshipped in Hill Cliff() Chapel August 20, 1648, was ill.. founded. He had just gone through a nerve- racking ordeal. His [Fray had cut up the King's forces which were being chased in two separate directions, and, as Mr. Beamont said, Croniwell would have other work on hand that Sunday morning than a country ramble to Hill Cliffe. The probabilities were that he listened to the Rector, Thomas Yates, who was a man after his own heart, at the :Warrington Parish Church which was near his headquarters in Church: street.

THE CUTTING AT HILL CLIFFE. Mr. Dunlop has made. some independent in- quiries as to the exact origin of the road that is cut through the rocks at Hill Chile. Mr. Pennington, of the Denna Farm, remembers the actual making of the road. The Waterworks Act is dated 1846, 68 years, ago. Mr. Pennington confirmed the ex- planation of the cut that states that when Appleton Reservoir was made nearly 70 years ago, the necessary stone was taken out of this rising ground, and the double object was achieved of getting the stone and making a diffi- cult road tolerably level. Mr. Dunlop's love of Nature asserting itself, he gave an interesting lecturette on the trees that are found growing out of the rock and over- hanging the road, giving the scientific explana- tion of the manner in which they not only establish themselves, but often crack the rocks in doing so.

BELLFIELDS. The House that Admiral Hoare Built. The party then made a move to Bellfields, a fine house only a few yards away from the "cut." The house which looks down the lane leading to the reservoir, and, standing high, has a splendid view of the water and the landscape that opens out beyond Walton, with the beginnings of the sea for a background, was built about 200 years ago by Admiral Hoare on his retirement from active service. It was planned like a battleship, but, as Mr. Dunlop stated, it has unfortunately, from an antiquarian point of view, been reno- vated in recent years.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
Planned Like a Ship. In Mr. Beamont's History of Appleton we have the following description, some of which is still applicable :

All the taste of Admiral Hoare in architec- ture was of a truly maritime type, and he set about building his house, not as an architect, but as a shipbuilder, he took a ship for his model, made cabins, officers' and warrant officers' rooms, and a number of other places, to all of which he gave nautical names, some of which were not easy for a landsman to pro- nounce. The grass plot before the house was divided from the lawn beyond it by a deep sunk fence, supported by a stout maintaining wall, which represented the vessel's side. The grass plot was his quarter deck, where his flag floated on the wind from a masthead, and there he walked regularly and took exercise at stated hours, the lawn and the fields beyond represent- ing the green sea, in which his ship was sailing and which so placed must have appeared As always riding on the crest of a wave. Church Union.

A special service for the members of the Eng- lish Church Union was held in St. Barnabas' Church on Wednesday, when the Rev. E. Davies, of Bradford, preached an inspiring sermon.

Temperance League. An open-air meeting will be held at the corner of ThelWall-lane and Knutsford-road on Wed- nesday evening commencing at 7.30. Councillor Henshall will preside and Mr. G. E. Virinterton (Manchester) will be the speaker.

"Co-op." Trip. The Warrington Co-operative Society's annual trip took place on Thursday, when upwards of 300 pleasure-seekers set out for Blackpool early in the morning. A most enjoyable day was spent, the party breaking up into groups, some of which visited St. Annes-on-Sea, Fleetwood, and the outlying districts.

Shortage .of Speakers. Owing to the demand for speakers by the land campaign in all parts, it has been found neces- sary to postpone the Monday meetings at Bridge Foot for a short time. Mr. Tom Wing, M.P., will address a meeting on Friday, July 24th.

Liberal Women's Picnic.. The annual picnic of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association will take place on Thursday, July 30th, when Blackpool will be visited. Amongst those who will make the journey will be Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield.

Old English Fair. Arrangements are being completed for the Old English Fair, to be held in Victoria Park on July 16th, 17th and 18th, commencing at 2.30 p.m. each day. The fair will have the assist- ance of all the different organisations of the Warrington ladies and gentlemen, and the funds will be devoted to the Infirmary and other institutions,

Monks, Hall Picnic and Bowling Main, The annual picnic and bowling main in con- nection with Monks, Hall and Co., Ltd. (Tube Department) took place on Saturday, when a party of 30 journeyed to Gateacre, where a main provided some capital sport. Thanks are due to Mr. J. W. Eccles (secretary) for the admir- able. way in which the arrangements had been carried out, the picnic being voted the best on record.

New Bowling Green. The new bowling green which has been laid to the Swan Hotel at Winwick by Mr. William Powell, of Fearnhead, was opened on Thursday afternoon, in the presence of a large number of Warrington and Manchester gentlemen, by Mr. T. S. Steel, of Warrington, who also bowled the first wood in a game of five up with Mr. IL Mather, Warrington.

Death of Rev. Samuel Wilson.

The Rev. Samuel Dobson, who was born in 1830 at Walton, near Warrington, died last week at Luton, where he had laboured in the Primitive Methodist cause for four or five years. Early in life he was engaged in the Northwich Circuit, was a missionary for several years in Warrington, and then, in 1865, went to Sheer- ness, followed by ministries in many other places, chiefly in the South.

Colleagues' Marriage Gift. The engineering department staff of the Pear- son and Knowles Coal and Iron Co. recently presented to Mr. Robert G. S. Finney, New- lands, Latchford, a very handsome clock, on the occasion of his marriage to Miss Bessie Evelyn Whitlow, Broomfields. A number of his colleagues also visited Stretton on the wedding day and gave him a hearty send-off, when he left for Fladbury, Worcestershire, where the honeymoon is being spent.

A Big Fire Avoided. The. efficient and excellent service of the Fire Brigade, combined with prompt handling by the employes, prevented what threatened to be a disastrous fire at the works of the St. Helens Cable and Rubber Co., Ltd., on Sunday evening. A message was received by telephone shortly after seven o'clock, and the motor engine and brigade, under Supt. Turner, were quickly jour- neying towards Bank Quay. In case of need the horses were hitched to the steam engine, but this was not called upon. The outbreak was in the most awkward part of the works, being in the "drying room," which is in the centre, and at the highest point of the buildings. The auto- matic sprinklers were working and a number of employes, assisted by workmen from the Aluminium Works, had rendered excellent ser- vice in " holding " the fire pending the arrival of the brigade. The brigade laid a hose up a flight of stairs and attacked the fire through a skylight in the roof. In the room involved a large stock of rubber, highly inflammable, was suspended. A good supply of water was ob- tained from an adjoining reservoir, and in about forty minutes the danger was over, but the men stood by for a time to make assurance doubly sure. So successful were the brigade, that their performance is considered the best recorded , locally for years as a feat of preventing a great conflagration. , The brigade were materially assisted by a body of policemen, who, under Inspector Foster, con- trolled the big Sunday night crowd that assem- bled in the vicinity of the threatened works.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY,

===== 0002601_19140620_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CRIPPLES DAY.

Pleasant Motor-Trip Through Delightful Country.

A HAPPY EXPERIENCE.

A long line of luxurious motor cars, laden with smiling children and gay with flags, was the sight that met the eyes of those who wit- nessed the departure of the Warrington crippled children on their annual outing, which took place on Thursday. Except for an hour's rain in the evening, the merry-makers were favoured with glorious weather, and one and all voted the outing the best so far. Although gratifying, this is not surprising, for the diligent labours of a zealous body of workers to make it so could hardly have been rewarded otherwise.

Early in the afternoon, the cars lined up in Bold-street, and, having picked up their occu- pants for the day, set forth along the prescribed route, viz.: Sankey-street, Bridge-street, Wil- derspool-causeway, Stretton, Acton Bridge, Cud- dington—turning to the right at the old toll-bar —and proceeding along the Chester-road to the Abbey Arms, to the National Children's Homes.

The Mayor and Mayoress witnessed the de- parture of the cars from the steps of the Wes- leyan Church. After a delightful three hours' ride through the beautiful country, the National Children's Homes, about l miles short of Frodsham, were reached.

An enjoyable couple of hours were spent in playing cricket, or in strolling about the grounds adjoining the beautifully situated homes, in the spacious schoolroom of which an excellent tea was provided. Tea having been partaken of by the workers,

the Rev. Hugh Williamson, on -behalf of the Cripples' Guild, proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the promoters and organisers of the outing. The Mayor of Warrington was giving a garden party to the Guild on the 30th of July. Mr. J. Paterson seconded, and Mr. W. Twiss, in supporting the motion, said Mr. W. H. Bel- lion particularly deserved their gratitude for his help. The motion was heartily carried. The motors returned to Warrington via Pres- to.

In Brook, having covered altogether 33 miles through lovely Cheshire scenery. The number of cripples carried was 141, an increase of 15 over the attendance at last year's outing, and we are glad to record that no accidents or break- downs occurred.

Valuable services were rendered by the Rev. Hugh Williamson, president; Mrs. A. Booth, secretary; Mr. W. H. Bellian, marshal; Messrs. J. Paterson and W. Twiss, organisers, with the president. The vice-presidents; Messrs. F. A. Harris, Frank Hickman, J. Whit:gore and J. Hamblett, with the assistance of the lodge com- mittee, also worked hard for the success. The officers and committee desire to express their thanks to the many ladies and gentlemen who kindly placed.motor cars at their disposal or contributed to the fund for the outing.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
MARRIAGE OF MISS GREGSON.

A quiet but interesting wedding was solera- nized on Thursday at the Parish Church, Huy- ton, between George Artingstall Williams, of Warrington, and Janet Sara, youngest daughter of the late, James Gregson of " Leckonby," Lymm. The bride was given away by her brother-in-law, Mr. Harold Speight, Mr. Wil- liam Milner, of Warrington, acting as grooms- man. The reception was held at "Mayfield," Huyton, the residence of the bride's sister. Mr. and Mrs. G. A. Williams left early in the after- noon for a tour in Devon and Cornwall.

_ Many useful and handsome presents were received.

Art Masters.

The National Society of Art Masters held their annual district meeting on Saturday afternoon , at the Warrington School of Art. Mr. H. Schroder, A.R.C.A.' of the Acton and Chiswick Polytechnic School of Art, president of the soc- iety, being present. After the meeting mem- bers had tea together at the King's Cafe, and subsequently visited the various buildings of interest in the town, Town Hall, Museum, &c. The new bridge works were inspected, through the kindness of Mr. Mansfield Cobb (resident engineer), and the design and construction fully explained.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "A Fool in Paradise."

The skilfully written play, "A Fool in Para- dise," is presented to Royal Court Theatre patrons this week in polished fashion by Miss Dora Eastlake's company. The " fool " is Dorian, sth Earl of Norton, whose affections are captured by Natalie Winter, a heartless adventuress, who uses him as an instrument by means of which to revenge herself upon an enemy of whom he does not know. So cruelly is he deceived, indeed, that the Earl's ultimate perception of his wife's true character causes the sacrifice of his reason, and only his devoted brother Jack can comfort him. Mr. Cecil Gray, the great emotional actor, impersonates the misguided Dorian in impres- sive and convincing style, whilst Miss Dora East- lake's cold, calculated portrayal of Natalie Winter is very effective. The genial Captain Jack Granby is presented' splendidly by Mr. Fred Charles, and Captain Francis Vivian by Mr. L. Ingham. Mr. Michael Raglan (Lieut. Reginald Dean), Mr. Patrick Branigan (Michael O'Connor), and Miss Ida Crofte (Lady Molly GranbY) supply humorous interludes appro- priately, and Amelia, Countess of Norton, is sympathetically impersonated by Miss Rita Rosslyn.

Next Week.

- The famous London West End actress, Car- lotta de Yonson, visits the Royal Court Theatre next week, and Presents the new and original drama, "The Great Detective," by C. Douglas Carlile.

CAPITAL HIPPODROME FARE.

Large audiences are assembling this week at the Hippodrome, where the chief item on a i capital hill of fare s a remarkably clever dis- play of diving by the Four Diving Norins. They perform with great skill in a tank of water only five feet deep, under the leadership of Miss Olga Norrin. A much appreciated visit is paid by Ray and Zack, from the Lyceum Theatre and the Coliseum, London. Dressed in extreme fashions—qie lady in a striking crinoline of vast proportions, and the gentleman quite up-to-date I—they render clever songs, and dance in a , finished fashion. "Burglars in Harmony," an is presented by the Mums o are vocalists of no mean sketch, of a different interesting ll' order; ee, dsi yts whilst ngiFgli given by ,ebt yc another will Sinclair and Whiteford. This is entitled " Kitty's Hero," and the plot, woven round a quarrel, is terminated in quite an ingenious and diverting way. A remarkable turn is given by Mr. Charles Santon, who, in addition to being a reciter, appears in the dual role of soprano and male vocalist. Lillie Den- ville, wooden shoe dancer and comedienne, and Will Sullivan, character comedian and patterer, do not fail to entertain the audience; and the same applies to " Hawkeye as Hall Porter" and "On their Wedding Eve," the Perfection pictures.

Next Week's Attractions.

Next week's fine Hippodrome programme in- cludes: The Record Quartet, spectacular novelty banjo act; Sara Williams, the famous prima donna; High Davos Co., trick roller skaters; Billy Spears, introducing "Lady," the clever cannie; the Two Georgis, in their hand balanc- ing act; Happy Attwood, the comedian who never smiles; Kendrick and Laura, entertain- ers; and the usual Perfection pictures.

ROYAL THEATRE OF VARIETIES.

The Royal Theatre of Varieties presents a splendid bill of fare next week, including:— Bugler Carr, in his sword manipulating act; the Sisters Brenton, vocalists and expert dancers;. and the pictures, "Facing Eternity" and "The Colonel's Wife," both featuring Henry Portern, upon the first and last three days respectively.

EARLY FAR,MI,NG CUSTOMS. Professor Walter J. Harte, University College, Exeter, in a paper he read before the Dairy Conference, said: The great expense of iron in the fifteenth century explains the fact that cart wheels were frequently made solid, cut from the section of a full-grown tree. Wooden ploughs and harrows with wooden pegs were used, and so the land was scratched rather than ploughed. The share of the plough must have ' been a very slight affair, having a wooden frame with an iron point to it. Steel was employed for the tips of the cutting edges of iron tools and was four times as dear as iron. Canvas had to be bought for mill sails and bags, and mill stones were a heavy item in a bailiff's accounts.

WARRINGTON CAPTAIN HONOURED.

loc baths on Tuesday, but the most important item was the Polo League match between War- rington and Bootle. Warrington defended the shallow end, but Bootle were early on the aggressive and Woods had no chance with a stinging shot from McLean. Warrington then pressed hard, and from short range Turner (captain) equalised with a good shot. The in- terval came with the score one each, Sisson and Graham hating had hard lines with shots that went just outside the mark. On resuming, Kay scored for Bootle, but again Turner equal- ised, receiving the ball from Graham and beat- ing the Bootle goalie in clever fashion. After many exciting incidents Kay scored a third for Bootle, but shortly before the call of time Graham made the score three each.

Mr. C. Turner, the Warrington captain, has been honoured by being selected to take part in the Lancashire County trial match to be played at Wavertree on Monday. After the match the teams will be chosen to represent Lancashire against Cheshire. Warrington play Southport in the first round for the Lancashire Cup in the local baths on Tuesday evening. A record attendance is ex- pected, as the match promises to be full of interest. We publish above a photograph of the local team, who are expected to bring honours to the town before the close of the season.

BEATEN AT LANCASTER. In a friendly match at Lancaster on Saturday, before a record crowd, Warrington were beaten by five goals to one. The game did not call for much comment, although some fine combination was shown by the Warrington team, but their play was loose when it came to attack. All the home team's goals were well merited. C. Turner scored a beautiful goal for Warrington. The visitors had a most enjoyable day at Morecambe, which included a bowling main, held at the Morecambe Hotel, the prize-winners being : 1, Mr. H. Sisson; 2, Mr. C. Woods; the score being 11-3 in Mr. Sisson's favour. After tea the whole party visited the R.M.S. Majestic, which is lying in Morecambe Bay, prior to being broken up in September. All the members of the party voted it was one of the most enjoyable outings ever held in connection with the club.

• Other Attractions. An exhibition race was given by H. Sisson and S. H. Fuller, of Ethyl, who is about to "sign on" for the locals. In the 50 yards' club handicap the following were the heat winners: W. Neil, H. Bennion, S. Ice, W. Sisson, H. Houghton, jun., T. Chant- ler, W. Caldwell, L. Graham, R. Weir, H. Ward, H. Silk, and J. Graham. In the first round of the Works' Competition the Borough Treasurer's Department beat the Post Office No. 2, and the Army Service Corps won over Pearson and Knowles No. 2. The semi-final and final of the Club Handicap, also the remaining races in the first round of the Inter-works Squadron will be decided on Tues- day, and the draw is: Crosfield's No. 1 v. Town Hall; Warrington Clerks v. Rylands' Bros., and Warrington Police V. Crosfield's Juniors.

A Difficult Situation But Both

Sides Firm.

STATEMENTS LAST NIGHT

The strike in the tanning trade was unaltered last night, when both sides issued statements to the effect that they had right on their sides, and intended to maintain those rights.

If the men's union have anything to put before them, I shall be glad to have such statement in writing not later than twelve o'clock on that day." i But !chile the union thus condemned the strike in their letter to the Masters' Association, in their addresses te the men the union officials plainly encouraged the strikers. Mr. Harrison, the organising secretary, said in the Co-operative Hall (see "Daily Citizen ") : " They had only to stand together and they would triumph. It could be practically said that the strike was officially recognised, though 1 the Executive Committee had yet to meet to give it their sanction." Mr. Ormsby, the secretary, spoke in a similar strain. Thus both in letter and spirit, the addresses of the union officials encourage a strike which, in their communication to the masters, they say they condemn. The masters fully recognise the right of the men to combine as trade unionists, but how is it possible satisfactorily to negotiate a perma- nent settlement with gentlemen who have given such clear evidence of their inconsistency, and who have destroyed all confidence in their ability to control their men in connection with a strike they assert to the masters they con- demn? _ _

Signed for and on behalf of the Master Tan- ners of Warrington. Runcorn and District, C. H. MITCHELL, Secretary.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MEN.

Mr. Harrison, speaking on behalf of the Strike Committee, told "The Examiner" reporter that his committee were prepared to meet the masters any time; they were sure they could justify the position they had taken up. Mr. Edward Ormsby, the general secretary, stated that the men, who were determined to hold out, had the hearty support of the men in Liverpool.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
A CONTRADICTION.

_ The Strike Committee ask us to contradict the report appearing in a contemporary stating that a man who was sent to prison was an offi- cial picket. The man was not even a member of the union.

MASTERS' STATEMENT. We have been supplied with the following statement by the Master Tanners of Warring- ton, Runcorn and district : Six months ago the Master Tanners' Associa- tion agreed to recognise the Tanners' Union. Joint committees were formed, and after several meetings, rates of payment (especially for beam work, that is, unhairing and fleshing) • were ar- ranged, approved, and embodied in an agree- ment. The agreement further laid down general conditions for the regulation of tanners' employ- ment, settlement of disputes, &c. This was signed and sealed by representatives of both organisations, stamped, and so made legal and binding. The masters have never been told by the Union officials, or the men, why the latter suddenly left their work, hence they can only get at the reasons by conjecture. They are of opinion that the following are possible reasons The agreement stated No. 16: " That the Tann'ers' Union agree to give two full weeks' notice before withdrawing , any men from their work."

South American Hides. On the Conciliation Board, which had been organised to settle questions arising out of the interpretation of the agreement, a difference of opinion arose as to the meaning of the agree- ment in regard to the payment for the fleshing of hides from South America, which the men contend is not included under clause 8 of the schedule to the agreement. At one yard the fleshers struck work without notice on this ques- tion, but after a time resumed work. The ques- tion was referred to the Conciliation Board, who could not agree, and the matter was referred to the properly constituted arbitrators. While the question was in this position, the men in the yard chiefly concerned again ceased work with- out notice. Non-Union Employes. Clause No. 20 reads: "The masters cannot agree to make any distinction between union men and non-union men in the works." An individual firm received official notice to the effect that unless the non-union men in their employ joined the union within fourteen , days the union men would cease work. On this being reported to the Masters' Association, this body considered the action of the union a gross violation of the agreement (see above clause), and notice was sent to the union intimating that if this notice was not immediately withdrawn the action of the union would force the masters to post lock-out notices to all union workmen to take effect after the required fourteen days. Instead of working out the union's notice of fourteen days, the men left work in four days. The officials of the union visited the yard in question, and as a result the men recommenced because the non-union men had joined the union. The Masters' Association secretary then received a letter from the union secretary, say- ing that they withdrew their notice to cease work on the distinct understanding that the Masters' Association withdrew their lock-out notices. The masters replied that the lock-out notices, which had been withheld, pending a reply, would not now be posted.

Question of Management. Mr. E. Ormsby, the union secretary, stated in the Co-operative Hall on June 9th, as re- , ported in the "Daily Citizen " "Inquiries elicited the fact that the em- ployers had been making preparations in the event of a strike. In various yards in Warring- tone veven ltihe ey Nvc.eeae ske db epf ourtet gThhied me s e inn klna es tw wt he eakt , suchand Procedure prevented success last September, and they were not going to stand by and see all their efforts frustrated without they could avoid it." In the above the case is not accurately stated. No employer ceased to Rut in hides, although one or two reduced their input. But what is of vital importance is that the union secretary here challenges the right of any employer, should he have reason to think there is a probability of his men going on strike, to safeguard his posi- tion by reducing the quantity of his goods, which are at special risk. The point is solely one of management, with which the masters cannot admit the men's right to interfere. This point is covered by rule 20 of the Con- ciliation Board. No. 20: "That the Board have no power to interfere with questions of discipline or man- agement." The position, which is clearly understood by both sides, is that in a tannery there is always a considerable portion of the animal products which are liable to suffer serious loss if there is any intermission in the processes_by_ a strike, or otherwise. In most trades a strike simply stopS production; in a tannery the most serious diffi- culty is the probable loss by the spoiling of the goods in process. The men's position is safe- guarded by clauses 17 and 18, which read No. 17: "The masters agree that in the event of notice being given on either side they will find the men work until the notice is worked out." • Note.—This was necessary on behalf of the piece workers, who might have finished their work before the fortnight had expired. No. 18: "The masters agree that in the event of notice being given on either side and the goods worked out, the men shall be found work within one week's time after settlement." The masters feel aggrieved that an agreement which was seriously, and apparently in good faith, entered into by the union on behalf of the men, should be violated both by the union and the men, and the agreement treated as so much waste paper. The masters are convinced if the policy of "down tools" becomes common, and takes the place of settlement by agreement, a condition of insecurity will be created that will be dis- astrous to the interests of the local tanning in- dustry, in which the welfare of both masters and men are concerned, and they are determined to establish this security whatever it may cost. Correspondence. When the strike materialised the secretary of the Masters' Association was instructed to write to the secretary of the union asking whether the action of the men in leaving their work had the sanction of the union, and whether it was the intention of the union to support those men morally or otherwise. The reply from the union stated "I can only say that my committee have con- demned the men's action of coming out without notice, and we have done our best to get the men to return to work. My committee are now compelled, in the 'interest of the masters and returne With that end in view my committee desires to support pwoorrtk necessary assooas possible. to enable n the men V e givet meet the Masters' Federation as soon as pos- sible to bring about an early settlement." The masters replied:— "You have proved by your letter that the union officials have no control over the men. It is useless to arrange the meeting you suggest at the present time." The union replied, June 16 : " Your letter of the 15th inst. to hand. Unless a meeting is arranged between the masters and the representatives of my union it is hopeless to bring the dispute to an end. The officials of the union have the confidence of the men and full authority to act on their behalf. I would be glad therefore if you would again consult the master tanners, and let me know if they would

.... meet us." The masters replied "In reply to your favour of the 16th inst.. there will be no meeting of the Master Tanners' Association until Monday next, the 22nd inst.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SCHEDULE.

We are asked to give the following from the " agreement " The First Schedule before referred to the Terms of Agreement arrived at between the Master Tanners of Warrington. Runcorn and District, and the Delegates of the Tanners' Union. on Monday, September 29th, 1913. 1. That the delegates call a general meeting as soon as possible, and that they recommend the acceptance of these concessions on condi- tion there is no victimisation. 2. That the delegates from the Tanners' Union pledge themselves that the Union will not support the men in any way who have come out without notice, and that they will urge the men to return to work immediately. 3. That 55i hours constitute a full week's work for day-wage men. 4. The masters agree to pay time and a quar- ter for overtime for day-wagers after a fully constituted week of 551 hours. 5. The masters are willing to concede the question of time and a half for bank holiday pay for day-wage work. 6. The masters agree to pay any piece-work men over 21 years of age sixpence per hour for any time on day work. 7. That all men be paid separately and direct by the firm. 8. That the piece-work rate for fleshing by hand be as follows: English hides, 791 b. and under, green weight, 20.; English hides, over 791 b. and up, green weight, 20.; dry and dry- salted hides, all weights, 2-0.; salted Conti- nental hides, 651 b. and under, re weights, .; salted Continental hides, over 651 b. up to 791 b, re weights, 20.; salted Continental hides, over 791 b. and up, re weights, 3d. 9. That the piece-work rate for unhairing and scudding by hand, included in one operation, be: English hides, 791 b. and under, green weight, 20.; English hides, over 791 b. and up, green weight, 3d.; dry and dry-salted hides, all weights, 20.; salted Continental hides, 701 b. and under, re weights, 20.; salted Continental hides, over 70Ib. and up, re weights, 3d.; South American hides, long shanks, 20. Further, where tanners require an extra scud- ding as a separate operation, they are entitled to pay extra price for this as before. 10. The masters agree to four pieces of offal being counted as one hide for fleshing and un- hairing. Fleshings at 20.; unhairing at 20. 11. The weights in the agreement re flesh- ing and unhairing salted hides refer to average landed weight. For market hides the tail weights are to be taken.., . .

12. For picking out white hair. 10d. per cwt. shall be the minimum rate loaded on to lurry. 13. Day-wage men. The masters agree to pay all able-bodied men 22 years of age and over, who have worked at least 12 months in the tan- nery, a minimum wage of twenty-five shillings per week. 14. The masters agree that where a man comes with a good character from another yard the 12 months' probation shall not be insisted upon before paying him twenty-five shillings per week. 15. The masters will obtain returns from ' each tannery as to the average wage for piece- work, other than for beam-men, for the last six months, and where these are admittedly low, will put them on a fair basis, and failing agree- ment the matter go to a Conciliation Board. Any increase given to date from the date of acceptance. 16. That the Tanners' Union agree to give two full weeks' notice before withdrawing any men from their work. 17. The masters agree that in the event of notice being given on either side, they will find the men work until the notice is worked out. 18. The masters agree that in the event of notice being given on either side, and the goods worked out, the men shall be found work within one week's time after settlement. 19. That a Conciliation Board be formed to settle disputes which cannot be settled amicably by the parties concerned. The deci- sion of the Conciliation Board to come into operation from the time of notice being given. 20. The masters cannot agree to make any distinction between union men and non-union men in the works. 21. The masters agree to wet hides in at once unon the acceptance of these terms by the union officially.

ASSAULT ON A CARTER.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Saturday morning, John Forster, 1, Mersey Mount, Mersey-street, was charged with assault- ing Abraham Betley in Mersey-street, on the previous day, and also with/committing, a breach of the peace. Defendant was represented by Mr. Linskey, of t..iverpool, and denied the offence. It was alleged that defendant stopped the rosecutor, who was carting for a tannery, held ae horses, and took the driver by the throat in a threatening manner. This was denied, and the defence was that the man was engaged in peaceful picketing and persuasion. A fine of 40s. and costs was inflicted, with the intimation that further cases would be severely dealt with.

"A STALE TRICK."

Scathing Criticism of the "Conversion"

Picnics to Ireland.

BAD USE FOR SURPLUS MONEY.

The " Manchester Guardian," in a leaderette last Monday, said:—

A Stale Trick.

Reputable members of the Opposition—who are, of course, no less abundant than reputable Liberals—would do their party a goo& service if they would induce some of its less serupulous agents to drop a manceuvre which verges on im- posture. The plan is to collect little groups of obscure persons who in return for a week's free holiday in Ireland will agree to sign their names to some string of abusive comments on the majority of Irishmen and of the more time-worn Conservative party phrases about Home Rule. The arrangement usually is that part of the squad is to call itself Liberals"" and another part "Labour men." The authors of the scheme then communicate the resulting document by telegraph to the Press, with a fine histrionic air of breathless haste and importance, and with the suggestion, direct or implied, that it repre- sents a remarkable and significant conversion of Home Rulers into Unionists by "contact with the realities or the Irish situation." Of course, no genuine and self-respecting Liberal or Labour man would accept the bribe of a free holiday in order to play a part in an anti-Liberal and anti- Labour comedy of this type. The wording, too, of all these " reports " betrays a strictly Con- servative party origin, and a pretty low one. There is never even an attempt to write in char- acter for the "Liberal" and "Labour" actors in the farce. Would it not be better to end the run of the farce? It imposes on nobody, it makes those who stage it look shabby, and it injures Conservatism by presenting it to the undeceived spectator as a party with any amount of money that is needed, and not much scruple.

THE KAISER'S HEIR.

Interesting details of the life of the Crown Prince of Germany are given in a book entitled The Kaiser's Heir." The following extract occurs in a chapter entitled " Prince Charm- ing" : "Fearless as an airman, the Prince is equally renowned as a dashing motorist and a daring rider—so much so, in fact, that, if rumour speaks true, he has repeatedly had to suffer confinement to his quarters in consequence of parental displeasure at his audacious escapades. , With black and white for his colours (those of Prussia) he has also started a racing stable— ' another quite new departure on the part of a Hohenzollern prince; and, in fact, he may al- most be said to have already done as much for Germany in the world of sport as his father in ' the domain of the sea. If William 11., by the creation of a Navy, has taught Germany how to swim, his son and heir has equally shown her how to shoot—especially to stalk, to play polo, lawn tennis, ice-hockey, golf, football, and the various forms of winter sports. "He has, in fact, constituted himself the patron and player of all kinds of English out- door games, and is the recognised leader of the new movement which promises to make Ger- many our most formidable rival—not only in the field of commerce, but also in the domain of sport, in which we„have hitherto been com- placent enough to regard ourselves as unsur- passable. No one more than the Crown Prince , has helped to break down the popular prejudice I in Germany against English forms of sport ' 'He will make of us,' a loyal admirer has already prophesied, 'the first all-round snorting people in the world.' IN THE COURTS.

CUUILftS (it aLsTON CASE.

Stop-watches I. Speedometers.

Charged with driving a heavy motor car at an excessive speed in Manchester-road, Wool- ston, John Isherwood Brierley, motor driver, 22, Norway-street, Seedley, Salford, appeared at the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday. He was represented by Mr. A. D. Dean, Liverpool (for the Commercial Motor Users' Association), and pleaded not guilty. The police evidence showed that.defendant was timed by the police on May 19 with stop- watcheS. 'He covered the distance of a quarter. mile in _one 'minute 6i seconds, which reprO- sented a speed of 13 miles 940 yards per hour.— Cross-examined, the police officers said they did not look at the speedometer on the vehicle be- cause they did not properly understand them. For the defence, Mr. Dean said that on the evidence of the officers, who said they did not understand speedometers, it would be almost a 'scandal if a conviction was recorded. For the officers to make such an admission astonished him more than anything he had ever heard in his connection with police court cases. The highest speed at which the car was going was registered for three minutes after the car stopped, and the one used on that particular car was perfectly accurate, and could be seen outside the court on the vehicle. The driver said he had been driving for 16 years,. and the only time he had previously been in trouble was when he was charged with a rear light offence. He was questioned about the speedometer, which he said was perfectly accurate. •He knew he was in a trap, for he saw one officer, who was wearing white overalls, reading along the road- side, and the others he noticed as he was passing ' them. He was only going at about 71 miles an hour.

The Chairman (Mr. F. Aylmer Frost) said defendant would be given the benefit of the d•Aibt, and the ease was dismissed.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

MONDAY.

Before Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Aldermen I Smethurst and Bolton, and Messrs. G. Pilling, W. Roberts, W. -Furness and T. Locker. The Poor Husband.—Elizabeth Ellen Cun- ningham, 19, Bank-street, was charged with assaulting her husband, Michael.—She pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable said that the charge had been reduced from one of unlawful wounding. The prosecutor was a fish hawker, and for some time he had been assaulted by his wife, who recently injured him in such a way that he had to go to the Infirmary. On Satur- day night the man went to bed at 10 o'clock, and some time later he had to take an enamel soup ladle away from her. About one o'clock in the morning she picked up an empty quart bottle and hit him with it, with the result that his ear bled very freely. When the police went to the house and told her she would be charged with the offence, she said, " It ought to be his throat."—Prisoner, who had nothing to say, and who had been before tile Court 67 times previously, was sent to gaol for a month.

TRAGEDY OF THE LINE.

Labourer Hooked Off a Wagon.

Mr. S. Brighouse conducted an inquest at Warrington on Wednesday concerning the death of John Wood, a general labourer, employed at the Viaduct Waggon Works, Earlestown, and who had resided with his wife at 12, Crown- street, Earlestown. Henry Wood, collier, 25, Mersey-street, Bur- tonwood, said deceased was his brother. When witness visited the Infirmary on Sunday night his brother said that would be the last time they would be together alive. He said he was knocked off the waggon by a hook, fell over the buffer and on to the line. Martha Jane Wood, the widow, said her hus- band said he was standing on top of some timber in a waggon, when the hook slipped and he fell.

James Woods, general labourer, 3, Princess- street, Earlestown, said he was working with Wood at the time the accident occurred. Wood was standing on some billets in a waggon. He was using the hook (produced), which he stuck into a billet, but as he was pulling, the hook slipped, causing him to fall over the end of the waggon. He struck another waggon, which was 2ft. Bin. away. Mr. S. Hird, H. 11.1, Inspector of Factories, asked if deceased was new to the work. Witness said it was his third day. They had only worked nine inches down the waggon, and it was therefore easy for the deceu.sed to fall backwards out of it. A nurse stated that death to place at 1.30 a.m. on Monday. A verdict of " Ace-Mental death" was re- turned, and the foreman expressed the jury's sYmpatlly with the relatives, a sentiment the Coroner endorsed. Mr. C. W. MR. P. M. OLIVER AND SMALL HOLDINGS.

Speaking at Morley. -Green, .T;a;" on Monday night, Mr. Oliver said the Liberal policy in regard to the land had a social and an economic justification. Small holdings meant a prospect and a prospect m;ant hope. It was hope more than anything else that made the world go round. Without hope many labourers could not carry on their work; without hope there would not be a prospective candidate in the kingdom. Small holdings, if properly car- ried out, also meant development. On the Ledsham estate, in Cheshire, since the initiation of small holdings, the cattle on the land had increased from 803 to 1,489, horses from 84 to 190, pigs from 181 to 690, poultry from 720 to 3,880, and the land now supported 432 persons instead of 98.

STOCKTON HEATH

TOWNSHIP TITTLE-TATTLE.

Those who were not '"Heathens" Became Heathenish when charged lid. By the car .conductor and then told They must walk from Stafford-road, Because "there was something wrong at the Bridge." Those who were already "Heathens" Became more, Heathenish. It is bad enough to have lid. fares, But worse when only a penny ride is given. Mr. Dunlop journeyed from Warrington To tell Hill Cliff e people all about Their glorious past. How contradictory we are. • One "Heathen" exclaims jeyfully : "What beautiful weather we are having !" Whilst another says it is too hot. The knuts and knutesses are satisfied, And startle the eye with their "daring" attire. The favourite walk past London Bridge Has been well patronised lately, And on Sunday there was a record crowd. Who Was the lady who ventured forth With a parasol this week? It was about the first that had been seen . During this "heat wave." Even a cow sought shelter From the scorching sun. But the London-road art dealer Would rather the beast had gone elsewhere. Such a diversion from dinner Was quite unexpected. We deny the statement That the cow saw beef on the table, And exclaimed "Alas! my poor sister," Because its artistic tastes Led it into the picture shop, Where it disabused the mind Of the.idea that " Sufis." Are the only picture mutilators. About Li worth of damage • Was done to the stock Before the beast could be backed out. A welcome sight has been The tar-sprayed. roads, Which the County Council have just com- pleted. We shall be spared the discomforts Of a severe "dusting" from the motors, no doubt, now. The Parochial Committee will meet on Tues- day, . But, we understand, it will not last long. These I.lmmer,evenings are so pleasant That Councillors want to enjoy them fully. The question of holidays Is troubling the heads of many "Heathens," And, judging from snatches of conversation, Wales seems to be a popular venue, Although many are going much further afield. June, "the marriage month." Does not seem much in favour with young "Heathens," For we have not heard of many taking place as yet. Of course, the month is not out, So perhaps we speak too soon.

Potato Market.—The annual Cheshire potato market, which is held during the summer on Mondays, Wednesdays ar4l Fridays for about eightf weeks, was commenced this week in a large field near London Bridge. The "Heathen" dealers generally anticipate record sales.

Council School Successes.—The results of the Lymm Grammar School scholarship examina- tions, which have just been published, include the names of the seven following scholars from the Stockton Heath Council Schools, of which Mr. F. Brook is headmaster: Denis Mounfield, Frank Cross, Edwin Gaskell, Reg,inald Chal- lenor, Evelyn Richards, Leonard Rigby, and Norman Peters. Nine of its scholars were called up for oral examination, and the school's average number of successes for the last 3 years is six—an extremely satisfactory figure when one considers that thp school has only been opened four years. The scholarships include fees for three years (X 22 10s.), travelling ex- penses, and also books where the children's cir- cumstances justify it.
