NEV ART "CRITIC."

Cow in a Picture Shop.

The family of Mr. J. S. Calvert, art dealer, London-road, had an alarming experience on Monday. Just as they were about to start din- ner a heavy tramping was heard, and it was ascertained, to the astonishment of all, that a cow had passed down the lobby and into the shop, where there are a large number of pic- tures. Emulating the Suffragettes, the eccentric beast damaged the pictures and made the place very disorderly. When it was being backed out it damaged other pictures on the floor. In addition to a small table being knocked over and a couple of bicycle lamps damaged, one of the pictures "mutilated" was found to have been pierced, by the animal's horns. The excit- ing incident was witnessed by a large number of People,

===== 0002601_19140620_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
IiNUTSFORD LIBERALS. LibCrals Deceived by Tories, But Have Not Wavered.

There was a large gathering last Saturday of Knntsford Division Liberals at Thornyeroft Siddington, by invitation of Mr. IT. N. Bickerton, vice-chairman of the association, who presided.

• Mr. Oliver Says Lords Will Go. Mr. P. M. Oliver, the prospective Liberal candidate for the linutsford Division, recalled that. at a garden party at Blenheim exactly three years ago the Tories first pledged them- selves to do what they could to help Ulster. If a strong line had been taken then by the Gov- ernment things might have been different now. Liberals did not, Verhaps, take it seriously. If three years ago Liberals were flippant with respect to the Conservative attitude, they were flippant because they never thought it possible that a party which in 1904 gave Ireland local government and in 1910 were thinking of Home Rule, would in 1914, rather than grant Home Rule, descend to crime. They never thought that a party which relied upon the money of Tariff Reformers would spend that money in importing arms to Ulster from no less a place than Germany. But though Conservatives had said fine words and though they tried to do fine things, Liberals had not wavered very much. The Home Rule Bill before long would receive the signature of the King.. (Applause.) There Might be an Amending Bill afterwards—(A Voice: "Keep it back. They're not worth it ")—but some of them perhaps did not very much care, because they b4lieverl that the Home Rule Bill wa,s a measure perfectly just to Great Britain, Ire- land, and Ulster. If the Amending Bill was passed it would be passed as a result of agree- ment and not of force. Home Rule having gone, the Lords must go too—lapplause)—tfecause since 1910 they had shown themselves less capable than before of exercising the most elementary duties of a re- vising chamber. When the House of Lords went, there must also go the land system which was bound up with it.

The New Land Campaign. Mr. F. Neilson, M.P., said he was glad to know that Mr. Oliver was to start a land campaign in the division on Monday. It was time land campaigns were started in earnest. They had talked about it for a long time, but the reason they had not started them well was because the people had not been quite certain what the Gov- ernment was to do in connection with the solv- ing of the land monopoly. They could rest assured, from the speech of the Chancellor of the ExclAuer in introducing the Budget, that the Government was determined to start at the root of the land question. He advocated the taxation of land values. A good many of the housing and other Acts had, in his opinion, been failures, because they had not attempted

•LIBERAL LAND SUMMARY.

Mr. Oliver, at Wilmslow on Tuesday night, summarised Liberal proposals as a minimum wage to promote the efficiency of the labourer, security of tenure to promote the efficiency of the farmer, rating reform to promote the effi- ciency of the landlord, and acquisition of laud as the ultimate resource of the community.

- ELECTRIC LIGHTING.

%Varrington Corporation's Application.

At the meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday the Warrington Corpora- tion intimated their intention to apply for a Provisional Order for supplying electricity for Thelwall, Grappenhall, Latchford Without, Stockton Heath, Higher Walton, and Lower Walton.

The Clerk said that at the last meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee it was resolved: "That subject to the Warrington Corporation applying for the order to supply Stockton Heath on the same terms as obtained in Warrington, they would npt oppose the Order."

The Chairman: If they get this Order they will have a monopoly in supplying these parishes. Have they agreed to supply at the same rate as in Warrington? The Clerk: That is so. You will have an opportunity of -going into the matter when thh application for the Order comes out. The Chairman: If they get the Order will it make it obligatory on them to 'supply those places? They might have a monopoly. The Clerk: They will make an obligatory area, I presume. Councillor Woodward: Are they coming to Kingsley? (Laughter.) Councillor Pilling: The other parishes will have something to say in the matter, I should think:

MOORE SEWERAGE SCHEME.

The Chairman of the Moore Parish Council wrote to the Runcorn Rural District Council this week stating that the extension of the Moore sewerage scheme was contemplated in the direction of Moss-lane. It was understood that the cost of this extension would largely exceed the sum sanctioned for these two schemes by the Local Government Board. He submitted for serious consideration "that before definitely adopting any further scheme, involving an addi- tional expenditure of money, the ratepayers of the township should be taken iirto the Council's confidence." It was suggested that a deputa- tion consisting of the Chairman and Clerk of the Parish Council be received with a view to a clearer understanding on the matter. The Chairman: I do not agree with that letter, because the whole of the sewerage scheme was bro,ught about' by the ratepayers of Moore themselves. They suggested the more extensive scheme.

The Clerk: Also the ratepayers have not been kept in ignorance, for the plans were put before the Parish Council.

On the proposition of Mr. Liner, it was de- cided that the matter be left with the Moore Sewerage Committee "to point out where the ignorance lay." ,

===== 0002601_19140620_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
DARESBURY.

SUNDAY SCHOOL DIFFICULTIES.

IA hat Teachers have to Face.

lIALF-CENTURV RECORD. •

• The difficulties with which the Sunday school teacher has to contend and a method of dealing with those difficulties formed the subject of an address delivered at Daresbury Parish Church on Saturday afternoon by the Rev. Canon Cogs- well (Rector of Wallasey) to a large number of members of and sympathisers with the Frod- sham Rural Deanery Sunday School Teachers' Association. The Rev. T. T. Lee-Jones (Vicar of Daresbury) conducted .the service, and he was assisted by the Rural Dean (the Rev. the lion. C. F. Cross) and the Curate of Daresbury (the Rev. W. S. Adams). Canon Cogswell, preaching from the words, "lie gave some as pastors and teachers," said that his interest in the Sunday school was very deep, for, during the last 51 years, hardly a Sunday had passed without his addressing the children in the church or in the school. Sunday school teachers had many difficulties, for in numerOus cases children did not go to school to be taught, as they had no idea of being taught 1 and did not wish to receive instruction. Their chief desire in attending the schools was to meet, have a good time, and to eat surreptitious sweets—a desire that had to be overcome. If the Sunday school superintendent would see that the children were arranged in classes and both 1 girls and boys marched in quietly to their seats, something would be gained, for he had generally. found that where there was disorder at the be- ginning there was more or less disorder all through the lessons. The trouble was the need for discipline.

UNRULY SCHOLARS. The preacher said that at one time he was clerical superintendent at a large boys' Sunday school, which was rather understaffed, with the result that a number of the scholars were un- ruly, and the place developed into a perfect pandemonium After a month's experience he began to observe which were the offenders, and, as an example, took one by the arm, and, after escorting him, said: "Go, now, until you can behave yourself, and then you can come back." These expulsions continued until about half of the scholars had been removed; then a hush fell on the remaining half, and the officials had no difficulty for the rest of the session. He was hardly prepared to advocate this measure, but he felt that instead of learning, these particular boys were preventing others from doing so. Surely it was better for half the school to learn than that the whole school should be upset by unruly boys. After a few Sundays, nine-tenths of the dismissed scholars came back by degrees, promising better be- haviour, and the school underwent a great change.

IF THEY ONLY HAD THE MONEY! The maintenance of discipline rested with the individual teacher, as it was impossible for the superintendent to keep order all over the school. It was, therefore, advisable to have as many class-rooms as possible. If they only had the money to follow the example of the day schools and give every class a room and its own teacher, then their difficulty with regard to dis- cipline would be nearly finished. To acquire personal and friendly acquaintance with the class, he suggested that the teachers should keep in touch with the parents. The Rural Dean afterwards welcomed the teachers to tea, which was served in the Sessions House and in tho Parish Room.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
JUNE 20, 1914.

LYMM.

Lymm Florist Honoured.—Mr. George Willan, the Nurseries, Lymm, has been elected a Fellow of the Royal Horticultural Society.

May Queen Finances.—We understand that the May Queen Festival Committee's efforts have been a success financially. A statement respect- ing this will be announced later.

On the Bench.—At the Petty Sessions on Wed- nesday there were on the Bench: The Hon. J. E. Cross (in the chair), and Messrs. T. J. Ridgway, T. H. Warburton, S. Gould, Holden, and J. Coxon.

A Lymm Artiste.—At a social in the Winmar- leigh Cafe, Warrington, on Monday evening, under the auspices of the recently organised Warrington branch of the Welsh National Society, Miss Mary Brundett, of Lymm, pleased all with her singing.

Bucklow Hill Congregationalists.—The anni- versary services of the Bucklow Hill Congrega- tional Church were held on Sunday, when the Rev. J. Johnson, of Asliton-on-Mersey, was the special preacher. The collections in aid of the church funds were about the same as last year.

Temporary Licence Granted.—Mr. T. S. Steel applied at the Petty Sessions for a temporary licence on behalf of Mr. William Allman, recently manager of the Lymm Conservative Club, to sell at the Church House Inn, Lymm, which was, until a short time ago, in the occu- pation of Mrs. Jane McCrede.-r-The application was granted.

Obscene Language.—John Mullin, farm lab- ourer., Agden, Lymm, was charged at the Petty Sessions with using obscene 'language in War. rington-road, Broomedge, late at night on May 30th.—It was stated that he had taken drink, but was not drunk.—Defendant, who had never been before the Bench previously, said he had only taken two glasses of beer that day.—Upon his promising not to repeat the offence, the case was dismissed.

• Refused to Quit—William Henry Kent, of the Farmers' Arms, Heatley, proceeded against John Wilson, farm labourer, of no fixed abode, at the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, for disorderly conduct on licensed premises, and refusing to quit.—Mrs. Fanny Kent proved the case. She said that Wilson came to her house at half-past seven in the evening of May 23rd, for a pint of beer. He was under the influence of drink, and consequently she refused to serve him. For half an hour he disturbed the neigh- bours.—Wilson, who had been summoned on the 23rd May and 2nd June for using obscene language, was committed for 14 days' hard labour.

New Club Manager.—Out of 72 applicants for the position of manager of the Lymm Conser- vative Club, a vacancy having been caused by the retirement of Mr. William Allman from that position, Mr. J. Quinn has been appointed, and has taken Up his residence at the club during the week. Formerly a dolour-sergeant of the Manchester Regiment, with 21 years' experi- ence, Mr. Quinn and his wife have for the last two years acted as steward and stewardess to the boys' camp at Prestatyn, under the super- vision of Councillor W. H. J. Jenkins, of War- rington.

Lymm Liberal Women's Picnic.—About 50 members of the Lymm Women's Liberal Asso- ciation journeyed to New Brighton for the an- nual picnic on Wednesday. The weather was ideal for the outigg, and after the trippers had wandered about the district, they all met at the Tower, where an excellent tea was_provided. In a short speech, Miss Welford, the energetic secretary, apologised for the absence of Mrs. Dodds, of Penketh, the president of the Asso- ciation. Amongst those who made the journey were: Mesdames Champ, Gotobed, Radcliffe, Wyatt, Edwards, Seymour, Baines, Bell, J. Bell, F. Guest, Johnson, Misses Welford, J. Holt, Mercer, Cheetham. The arrangements were most complete.

Offending Cyclists.—At the Petty Sessions several persons were summoned for riding bicycles and other vehicles without lights.— Frank Gleeson, 25, Church-street, Warrington, pleaded guilty to having ridden his bicycle on the evening of May 2nd at Lymm without a light. It was stated that prisoner had had his lamp stolen at Knutsford. He was fined 3s. 6d., in- cluding costs.—Hubert Horace Stones, student, Heatley, Lymm, was charged with driving a motor car at Bollington on 17th May without a lamp over the real- number plate. Laurence Allen, of Bowdon, stated that he saw defendant set out from Bowdon a short time before with his lamp lighted. He was fined ss. and costs.— William Lomas, carriage builder, 12, Pierpoint- street, Warrington, was fined 55., including costs, for riding a bicycle without a light at Booth's Hill on June 4th, and Joseph Dowson, clerk, New Moss-road, Cadishead, was fined ss. including coats, for a similar offence at Rush -Green on the same day.—Robert Grace, carter, 134, Knutsford-road, Latchford, drove a lurry in Lymm-road, Warburton, on May 14th without a light and was fined 2s. 6d., including costs.

THE NEXT ELECTION. Invitation to Newtown Liberal.

Liberals of the Altrincham Division are pre- paring to contest the seat at the next election. Mr. Edward Powell, of Plas-y-Bryn, Newtown, Montgomeryshire, has been invited to address a special general meeting of the Altrincham Division Liberal Association with a view to his adoption al the Liberal candidate. Mr. Powell, who is a well-informed politician with strong Liberal sympathies, has displayed great interest in public work. He is a promi- nent member of the Montgomeryshire County Council, and the chairman of the County Insur- ance Committee, while he is a magistrate for the county. Mr. Powell is also chairman of the Humber Motor Company. In the January and December elections of 1910 Mr. Powell contested the Oswestry Division against Mr. W. C. Bridgeman, M.P.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
WESLEYAN ANNIVERSARY

Not a Selfish Religion. CIIRIST'S LESSON.

The Sunday school anniversary of the Lymra Methodists was celebrated on Sunday? when the special preacher was the Rev. R. 01. Hutchinson, of Headingley College, who is well known in the district.

He delivered an interesting address at the morning service; and in the afternoon he gave a short address to the scholars of all the Noncon- formist bodies in the district, who assembled, as usual, at the Lymm Wesleyan Church. He preached at the evening service from Matthew xxvii. 42: "He saved others; Himself He cannot save. If He be the King of Israel let Him now come down from the Cross, and we will believe Him." It was customary in these days, he said, to sympathise with people who were in trouble, so that they could scarcely realise that the words he had quoted were spoken to a dying man. Nowadays sympathy was offered even to those who were the cause of their own undoing, but at the time the text referred to it was quite customary and usual to taunt and mock men condemned at the hands of the law. It was true that Christ saved others and that He could not save Himself, but ves it was not true in the way the Phariseees meant. saved wasHimse utltferal held in Christian' life and thought. If He had done so He would have left a moral stain, and no longer would they have their perfect man. utterly impossible ibltehe for placeilim"H t He a One thing was clear—Christ endeavoured to win men to believe and follow Him, and with that end in view He made great sacrifices. The Jews expected Christ to come as a supernatural being, and if He had come down from the Cross they would certainly have believed. The lives of truly great men had been made by way of the Cross. They, had not sought "self," but had lived for others, and their chief joy had been in making others happy. During the day the children and choir ren- dered special selections, Mr. Harry Brown pre- siding at the organ.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
TEA AND ENTERTAINMENT.

...... The anniversary celebrations were continued on Monday evening, when about 200 assembled at the schools for tea. Immediately the tea was concluded an entertainment was given. This opened with a eantata, entitled "Cloud and sunshine," which was pleasingly produced. The

musical items in the programme were very creditably rendered by the Misses Hilda and Maggie Winstanley, Winnie Mairs and Jessie Pearson, Mrs. Margetts, and Messrs. Brazendale (conductor), Walter Perrin, and William Hinton.

The collections and donations and sale of tiekets have brought the total up to date to Xl3 us.

HIGH LEGH.

Cricketers' Success.—The High Legh cricket team entertained Sale Moor last Saturday and sent them home defeated. The hobo team com- piled a total of 143, of which Mr. Surridge secured 46, whilst Sale Moor could only reply with 81 runs.

HIGH LEM SPORTS.

Full List of Prize.winners.

The beautiful velvety sward of the High Legh Cricket Club gives quite ideal conditions for the village juveniles in which to fight their battles to gain supremacy in sport. The foliage was looking its best on Thursday, when the annual village• sports were held, and although the weather was not very promising, a large crowd assembled.

It is very difficult to state how long the fes- tival has been in existence, but praise is due to Mr. H. Tunstall, the hon. secretary, who for 38 years has laboured for the cause, helped by efficient and enthusiastic workers.

Early in the afternoon the villagers' children marched from their school, through the private grounds of Col. Legh, to the park, where the sports were very soon in progress. Later there was public dancing to the strains of the Lymm Subscription Band.

GRAPPENHALL.

Policemen's Visit.--On Thursday afternoon the Warrington police journeyed to Grappenhall, at the invitation of Mr. A. Lister-Kaye, to play a friendly game of cricket with the local team. After a very exciting finish the police ran out victors by 10 runs, the scores being: Police 56, Grappenhall 46. For the home side E. Banks scored 14, whilst P.C. Stuart made 21 and P.C. Dixon 18. Mr. G. C. Hutchings took six wickets for 29 runs. After the match the police and their wives were entertained by Mr. Lister-Kaye at the Parr Arms, where an excellent repast was provided by Mr. J. Onions.

TWO DUSTY ROADS.

Grappenhall's_ Protest to District

Council.

At a meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday a resolution from the Grap.. penhall Parish Council was read: " That the Counoil be requested to tar spray Albert-road and Victoria-avenue.

Councillor Lister-Kaye said a complaint was brought before the Parish Council with refer- ence to the dust being so dreaslful that no one could go up the roads when there was a wind. The Clerk: You have just had those roads made under the provisions of the Private Street Works Act. They should not be so bad. , Councillor Lister-Kaye: We had a complaint before us the other night. Councillor Linaker : What traffic is there?, Councillor Lister-Kaye: There is practically no traffic. Councillor Mclntyre: It is a gradient. Mr. Wright (surveyor) : The roads are not finished yet, and the contractor is not likely to finish them for some time. I think the sugges- tion of the Parish Council is a good one if carried out. On the proposition of Councillor Lister-Kaye, it was decided that the contractors be asked to get the work through as soon as possible.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH.

Wesleyan Anniversary.—The annual anniver- ,sary sermons were preached on Sunday at both morning and evening services, and a musical service was given in the afternoon, under the direction of Mr. George Carman (choirmaster). The Rev. F. W. Kernick, late resident minister, was the preacher for the day, and Mrs. R. E. Garnett presided in the afternoon. Speaking of " happiness," the preacher said that the sun- shine of God was the great secret of real happi- ness and success. At the musical service a varied programme occupied the whole of the afternoon, solos, duets, anthems and choruses being admirably rendered. A well-deserved tri- bute was paid to Mr. Carman and all those who took part in the musical portion by the Chairman, who acknowledged the time and trouble necessary to obtain such high results. In the evening the text was taken from Numbers xiii. 30: "Let us go up at once and possess it, for we are well able to overcome it." The ser- vices were well attended throughout the day. The collections, which are devoted to the Sun- day school, amounted to £26 15s. 5d., which is slightly under the desired amount, but satis- factory considering the unsettled state of the district.

Tannery Band's Success.—On Saturday the Penketh Tannery Prize Band journeyed to Bold Venture Park, Darwen, to compete in the contest promoted by the Darwen Brass Band Commit- tee. Before a record attendance, in grand weather, 15 bands plated for the Industrial Co- operative Society's Challenge Cup, valued at 215 155.; the "Bentham" Challenge Shield, valued at .221; and the "S. J. Fish" Challenge Cup, valued at .212 125.; also cash prizes to the value of .225 10s. After some keen competition and keener criticism, Nelson Old Band were awarded first place, Thornsett second, and Penketh third, in the "Selection," and Horwich first and Penketh second in the quick- step. A special prize, presented by a local merchant, consisting of a gentleman's suit case, valued at 10s. 6d., for the best solo euphonium player, was awarded to D. Massey, of the Tannery Band.

SHAKESPEARE'S COUNTRY.

STRATFORD-ON-AVON.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
A Typical ,English Town and its many Interests.

THE HEART OF ENGLAND.

Warwickshire is at the heart of England, and at the heart of Warwickshire is Stratford-on- Avon. So, at least, most Englishmen and all Americans would agree, and it cannot be a mere coincidence that the Englishman of whom we are most proud was born there. To take us on a long pilgrimage through one of the sweetest of English counties, to make calls at its towns and castles and ancestral homes, to linger long enough, to show us beauty and teach us history, yet not long enough to weary us, is the task undertaken by Archdeacon Hutton in a hook just published, "Highways and Byways in Shakespeare's Country" (Mac- millan, 55.). The volume is a labour of love which must have given its author unmeasured happiness, and even those who may have envied him the happy task will be constrained to admit that none could have performed it better.

ing from under an exceedingly dirty cap. She was peculiarly assiduous in exhibiting the relics with which this, like all other celebrated shrines, abounds. There was the shattered stock of the very matchlock with which Shake- speare shot the deer on his poaching exploits. There, too, was his tobacco-box, which proves that he was a rival smoker to Sir Walter Raleigh; the sword also with which he played Hamlet; and the identical lantern with which Friar Laurence discovered Romeo and Juliet at the tomb! There was an ample supply also of Shakespeare's mulberry tree, which seems to have as extraordinafy powers of self-multiplica- tion as the wood of the true cross."

Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

When the rear axle of their motor car was damaged, a party of Englishmen who are ex- ploring the dark continent from the Cape to Cairo were saved from disaster through the possession of a motor cycle: One of the party, mounting the "single-tracker," aarted back- ward over the jungle paths for an outpost .of civilisation, where repairs could be obtained, eventually returning in triumph with the neces- sary parts.

Austrian Inventors Make Teeth. Machines for casting artificial teeth of various metals have been open to objection in that uniform density could not be obtained, and defects developed owing to lack of proper con- trol of pressure. Two Austrian -engineers have lately constructed a device at Prague, which, it is claimed, eliminates such defects.. The pressure is automatic, always uniform, and is exerted vertically, something heretofore re- garded as impossible in casting the molten metals in such small moulds.

Prison Farm in New Zealand. thew reformative stagene prison reform will be reached ill•eformative prison-farm at Waikeria (New Zealand) is completed. The farm consists of about 1,200 acres, a good deal g of which is already bein_ cultivated under A n important when plans laid down by the manager of one of the Government experimental farms, and it is intended that the institution shall be used for prisoners of the better type, with provision for 300 persons.

Paint from Beans.

From Manchuria comes the invention of a new paint that is both waterproof and fireproof in addition to being cheap and durable. " Solite" is the name of this new product, and three months after its introduction it found a ready market in Shanghai, Harbin, and Tient- sin. It is reported that the Japanese Navy is testing it with a view to adoption. "Solite" is made from bean oil, which is produced in tremendous quantities in Manchuria.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
Heat Received _bY Earth from the Sun.

One square yard of the. earth's surface re- ceives each day averaging six hours of sun- shine an amount of heat equal to that contained in I.Blb. of coal, according to an Italian chemist. At this rate one square mile would receive each day heat equivalent to the combustion of more than 2,600 tons of coal; or during the year an area of about 1,200 miles an amount of energy from the sun equivalent to the 1,100,000,000 tons of coal and lignite mined annually in the United States and Europe.

0 Faking " a Cinema Thriller.

_ A recently produced "movie" film shows a daredevil detective using a dirigible to overtake an express train in which is riding, in fancied security, the criminal who seeks to escape him. The picture shows the detective climbing down a knotted rope from the dirigible to the roof of the speeding train. Pew scenarios have called for a more hazardous feat. When shown upon the screen it is so realistic that the spectators involuntarily clutch at their chairs, scarcely breathing until the act is completed in safety. An article in the May "Popular Mechanics Magazine" explains how the scene was "faked."

A Cure for Warts.

The Budapest correspondent of the "Lancet" states that Dr. Felix Szontagh, of that city, has discovered a new and simple way of treat- ing warts. It consists in covering the wart and the surrounding skin, to the width of one centi- metre, with ordinary caoutchouc plaster, and leaving it so covered for weeks—even, if neces- sary, for two or three months. Whenever the plaster becomes loose it must be removed and replaced by a new piece. As a rule the plaster is to be changed once in three or four days. Any adherent residue of the caoutchouc is to be 'removed with ether or petrol, care being taken that tile wart or the surrounding skin shall not be ,rubbed energetically, because irritation of the skin is not conducive to the disappearing of the wart.

The Inventor of Income Tax. Few guess that the Income Tax, which figures so prominently in the present Budget, was devised by one of those "dons" of Oriel College, Oxford, whom Cecil Rhodes styled in his will "children in matters of business." It was suggested to Pitt by Dr. Henry Beeke, a 'Fellow of Oriel, who became Dean of Bristol. Beeke was Professor of Modern History at Oxford (says the "Westminster Gazette"), but his real forte was finance, and he had a hand in framing all the Budgets of Vansittart during that dull barnacle's long tenure of the Chancel- lorship of the Exchequer. Beeke's chief work, "Observations on the Produce of the Income Tax," has been warmly praised as "the best application of statistical reasoning to finance." It gives the most lucid and accurate account of the economic condition of this country at the beginning of the last century.

" Plant i ng " Hairs in the Scalp. According to The Times," a method of sew- ing hairs in the human scalp in cases of partial or total baldness has been successfully used in several instances by Dr. Szekely at the hospital of St. Stephane, at Budapest. The numbers of hairs " planted" in the head of a patient has been as many as 50,000. One hundred hairs are drawn through punctures in the scalp to every square centimetre, and as both ends are left free, the number is thus 200, or over 1,000 to each square inch. Very fine gold wires are used, 1-500th of an inch in diameter and fine long hairs from a woman's head are attached at the middle to these. It is stated that 500 hairs can be thus introduced into the scalp within three-quarters of an hour. Dr. Szekely has designed a special instrument for introducing and fixing the gold wires. The latter are so light and so fine that the total amount of the gold in the scalp after." planting" 50,000 hairs h is only one gramme. The hair is stated to appear perfectly natural, and a capsule of tissue appears to form around each gold knot. The hair can be washed, ruslied, and treated with oil in the ordinary way, and one of the earliest patients so treated, a lady, has retained her .hair intact, with the original lustre and flexibility, for over seven years.

Uric acid and poisonous waste are always get- ting into the blood from your food and from the wear and tear of the body. But the kidneys filter it out and keep the blood pure. Every 24 hours they remove about 500 grains of urinous poisons and 3 pints of water from the blood.

But when the kidneys are weak, they leave some of this poisonous waste in the blood and it gets carried to all parts of the system. It makes you feel dull, heavy and drowsy; your back as bad, your head bad; dropsical swellings may appear in the ankles or about the eyes; you may get rheumatic twinges, lumbago attacks, or dirz3 spells. ===== 0002601_19140620_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
In summer as a guide book, in winter as a fireside holiday, it will be prized and re-read. The skilful unveiling of the past—a process so difficult of accomplishment, yet so delightful

when achieved—can make any corner of Eng- land interesting. How much so a town' which has the most complete set of municipal records in existence, and which gave to the race its greatest and most human poet

That Stratford is an interesting town, apart from Shakespeare, every historian will hasten to agree. Its name, to begin with, is a bit of fossil history, for Stratford (Street Ford) tells that the Roman road here crossed the Avon, but of the road or its makers we should have known nothing but for the name. It appears in the Domesday survey, and its mill brings to the Bishop of Worcester ten shillings a year or a thousand eels, and the businesslike ecclesiastic manages to make his property worth twenty-five pounds a year to him. Various trades begin to make their centres there, and by the middle of the thirteenth century its place in the district is assured. It has a market and a cattle market in the thirteenth century, and fairs follow a little later. Ecclesiastical importance came in the fourteenth century with the founding of a chantry, which afterwards became a college of priests. To this we owe the exquisite church in which Shakespeare was laid to rest.

SOME OLD INSTITUTIONS. One benefaction to the collegiate church is noted by Archdeacon Hutton. It is one for four choristers, who were to sleep in a special bed- chamber in the church, "so hereunto they wereto repair in winter at eight of the clock, and in summer at nine; in which lodging be two beds, wherein they were to sleep by couples; and that before they did put off their clothes they should all say the prayer of De Profundis with a loud voice, with the player and orisons of the faith- ful, and afterwards say thus: God have mercy on the soul of Ralph Collingwood, our founder, and Master Thomas Basal, a special benefactor of the same."

The Stratford " Guild of the Holy Cross, the Virgin and S. John Baptist" gives us another glimpse of old English life and habit. It dates from the early thirteenth century and received the gift of a chapel.

It had a guildhall, with parlour, counting- house and school, built in the fifteenth cen- tury, which also remain. To the Grammar School of the Guild, built in 1427, Shakespeare went as a boy; in the Guild Hall he is said to have first seen a company of strolling players, and he died under the shadow of the Guild Chapel. The Guild was secularised in 1553, the Mayor, Aldermen and Council taking its place and taking over ip property.

HOW STRATFORD WOKE UP. Not until the eighteenth century did Strat- ford discover that it had given a genius to the world, and then it had to be told by an outsider. It was Garrick who made the town famous by arranging in 1769 what was called the "Jubilee." Careful not to place too great a strain upon the intellect of Stratford, Garrick arkanged a varied and recreative programme. Starting them off with a breakfast at the Town Hall on the Wednesday morning, he introduced the Oratorio of Judith, given in the church, a mas- querade, the recitation of an ode on Shakespeare given by himself in an amphitheatre erected on the river's bank, a band of music to parade the streets, fireworks at night and a horse race for a silver cup. The programme concluded on the Friday evening, and it is pleasant to learn that Garrick's endeavour to honour Shakespeare was crowned with wonderful success. The promise of three crowded days of glorious life drew visitors from far and near. Beds could not be found for them, and even distinguished ones were " constrained to sleep in their carriages." Thus happily made aware that it is a famous town, Stratford has never allowed the world to forget it. In the matter of relics, the inhabitants did their best, as they also began to do in the matter of stories and traditions. Washington Irving, who sought out the house reputed as the poet's birthplace, thus immortalises the lady who showed him round : " A garrulous old lady with a frosty red face, lighted up by a cold, blue anxious eye, and gar- nished with artificial locks of flaxen hair, curl.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
that John Shakespeare bought it in 1575. It is certain, also, that he lived in Henley-street in 1552, because in that year he was fined for hav- ing a midden in front of his house—a result of keeping pigs—but whether his famous son was born there in 1564 will probably never be known. Still, having seen the old house, we feel that we cannot do without it. It is a good example of an Elizabethan homestead, and Shakespeare , must certainly have known it, and spent some lof his early years within its walls. There is no original furniture, and we must imagine the truckle-beds, joint stools, table-boards, spinning- wheel, pails, pots, wood and leather bottles, pot hooks, gridiron, kneading troughs and candle- sticks. But the fireplaces and chimney corners with the hooks that held the bacon while it smoked in the fumes still remain, and as relics of Tudor architecture they are worth not a little. And, again, as the Archdeacon reminds us, whether the house be Shakespeare's birthplace or not, "it has the ' aura ' of centuries of de- votion." It has been the Mecca of notable men and women for several generations, whose horn. ago alone would make it the most famous home- stead England can boast.

SHAKESPEARE'S SCHOOL. Yet Shakespeare is brought nearer to us by the group of buildings that cluster round the Guild Chapel. Here is the schoolroom in which he learned his "small Latin and less Greek"; be- neath it is the hall in which the strolling players first fired his imagination. Not twenty yards away is New Place, where he spent his last years and where he died. From his garden or win- dows Shakespeare could see the boys with their satchels and shining morning faces creeping like snails unwillingly to school, or running home 'with shouts of glee—a perpetual reminder of lighter days. They still foregather there.io get what inspiration they can from the remem- brance that Will Shakespeare sat un'der the same rafters and gazed through the same win- dows when tasks were dull and the hours seemed long.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TWELVE-FOOT PARLOUR.

Of Stratford inns and the noble guests they have sheltered—not forgetting that Shakespeare himself must have familiarly known the in- terior of at least one of them—quite a little chapter might be written. It was of one of them that Washington Irving wrote:— "To a homeless man, who has no spot on this wide world which he can truly call his own, there is a momentary feeling of something like independence and territorial consequence, when, after a weary day's travel, he kicks off his boots, thrusts his feet into slippers and stretches him- ' self before an inn fire. Let the world without go I as it mar; let kingdoms rise or fall; so long as he has the wherewithal to pay his bill, he is for the time being, the very monarch of all he surveys. The armchair is his throne, the poker his sceptre, and the little parlour, sane twelve feet square, his undisputed empire. It is a morsel of certainty, snatched from the midst of the uncertainties of life; it is a sunny moment gleaming out kindly on a cloudy day: and he who has advanced some way on the pilgrimage of existence knows the importance of husband- ing even morsels and moments of enjoyment. Shall I not take mine ease in mine inn?' thought I. as I gave the fire a stir, lolled back in my elbow-Chair, and cast a complacent look about the little parlour of the Red Horse, at Strat- ford-on-Avon." We can imagine no happier holiday than a trip in Shakespeare's country, by motor or the more modest cycle, or even without either. Archdeacon Hutton has given us a perfect guide book, the twelve-foot parlour waits for us. Nothing more, indeed, is needed, except "the wherewithal to pay the bill."

===== 0002601_19140620_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
AVERAGE DURATION OF LIFE.

A Steady Increase. Mr. Herbert Samuel, replying in the Parlia- mentary papers to Mr. King regarding the esti- mated average duration of life in the years 1904, 1909, and 1914, in respect of the total population, said that two life tables, based respectively upon the mortality in England and Wales in the ten years 1901-1910, and in the three years 1910-1912, are in course of preparation. These tables show provisionally that the expectation of life at birth, based upon the mortality in the ten years 1901-1910, was 48.53 years for males, 52.38 years for females, and 50.42 years for all persons irrespective of sex. In the three years 1910- 1912 the expectations were 51.50 years for males, 55.35 for females, and 53.38 for all persons.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
AMATEUR PHOTOGRAPHY.

Warrington Society's Trip to Frodsham and Helsby. THE TRIPOD STAND.

" Practicus" writes in the "British Journal of Photography" : A remarkable amount of inventive genius has been lavished upon that simple appliance, the tripod stand, and many and varied have been the patterns ,placed before photographers. Yet after nearly threequarters of ft century we are still looking for the perfect instrument. The desiderata in a tripod are: perfect steadiness, great range in height without undue spreading of the legs, a fairly large head convenience and strong screw fittings and reasonable lightness. There have been many devices for preventing a tripod from slipping—some ineffective, and some clumsy, but otherwise good. In the first category must be placed all such devices RS corks and rubber pads on the points of the legs, as well as nearly all arrangements of cords. The other class may be exemplified by the triangular or star shaped frames which may be laid upon the ground or floor, having holes or recesses into which the legs will fit. I have found three strips. of wood, the length of the stand when folded, hinged together at one end, to answer every purpose, permitting the camera to be moved about without disturbing the levelling. Mr. F. H. Evans uses such a device for his cathedral work, -so that further commendation is not necessary-. An important point in tripod manipulation is steadying the apparatus in windy weather. I /have found nothing to equal the strap of the carrying case, with one end formed into a stirrup for the foot and the other attached by a screw through one of the buckle holes to the ! tripod head. It should be adjusted to such a length that when the foot is put into the loop it should require a fair tension to hold it to the ground. This is much better than the india- rubber spring arrangement sometimes recom- mended, as it gives a more dead pull with only just enough elasticity. Whenever there is the slightest doubt as to the stability of the stand, it should be anchored by hanging some heavy object on the leg which is likely to lift. In default of anything better, the case holding the slides will generally be found sufficient.). -

===== 0002601_19140620_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
TONING P.O.P.

Hymns Sung By Waiting Women. Two hundred miners were rescued this week from Cawdor Pit, Garnant, near Swansea, after having been entombed 20 hours. A fall of roof had cut off their escape. The rescuers managed to communicate with them through an aperture, but during the night this closed up. The efforts to get at the men were increased, and mean- while women and others on the pit bank sang hymns.

For a considerable period I have had in suc- cessful use a method of treating p.o.p. which is entirely difierent from that recommended by all makers of p.o.p. and the authors of the best known text books. The method reduces the time required to finish the print after removal from the frame by about one half, or less, and the finished prints are certainly in no way inferior to any that have been made under the universally-prescribed method. I hay.e searched all the printed in- structions I can put my hands on; and, without exception, they advocate a preliminary washing of the prints for, in some cases, as much as a quarter of an hour before proceeding to tone. By my new method this preliminary washing is not required. The prints are put straight into the toning solution, which consists of gold chloride and ammonium s.ulphocyanide in the usual proportions modified in the simplest man- ner. The modification on which all turns consists of nothing more than the addition to the ordin- ary toning solution of a small quantity of cora- mon salt. Tile exact amount to be added may vary to an almost indefinite extent; but as a guide I may say that a slightly heaped teaspoon- ful of salt to a pint of toning bath will be quite near enough. I abstain from giving any exact weights, as there is no necessity for elaborate precision. No milkiness occurs, the prints be- gin to tone at once, and a whole batch may be toned and ready to fix before, according to the old plan, they would have been sufficiently washed for toning. There are, so far as I can see, absolutely no objections to the method. A grain of gold will tone just as many prints in this way as it will

She says that very old ladies Don't care so much what they eat; So she's let me choose the goodies We're to have at the garden treat.

I chose some cold plum-pudding. And some damson-tart and milk; And Dolly shall come to the party In her very bestest Silk.

PRESENCE OF MIND. rtion of the wall near the dome with paintings '. It was desirable to have the ' During the re-building of St. Paul's Cathe- dral, in London, a famous artist was engaged 'toitdhecpoaraintetina P 3 whole church completed soon, and the artist began his work while the joiners and plasterers were still in the building. Mounted upon a scaffolding, similar to those which supported the workmen around him, he set to work. One day, while he was painting a religious subject of great interest and beauty his mind grew so full of the study of the picture that he quite forgot where he stood. He stepped back from the wall in order to get a better view of the picture. Step by step he moved back- ward, with his eyes fixed upon the painting, until he stood at the edge of the platform. An- other step would carry him beyond it, and send him 'crashing to the floor two hundred feet below. At this moment one of the workmen who were near him happened to look up and saw the peril in which the painter stood. He was about to cry out to the latter, but remembered just in. time that to do so might startle him and hasten the dreaded end. In an instant the , workman saw what he could do, and he did it. He ran to the painting, caught up a brush loaded with paint, and dashed it against the most beautiful part of the picture. It was enough. Blinded with rage, the painter rushed upon the man, and began to beat him furiously. Instead of striking back the man laughed, and said: " I do not mind your beating me, now that you are saved. I am sorry to have spoilt your picture, but it was the only way I could save your life." These words recalled the painter to his senses and he fully understood the terrible danger in which he had stood. He thanked his preserver again and again, nor was his promise a vain one, for during the rest of his life he performed many acts of kindness to the workman and his family.

RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

Question of Old Age Pensions.

BOARD'S DECISION.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presiding.

NEW MEDICAL OFFICER. Four applicants for the position of Medical Officer and Public Vaccinator for the Runcorn District appeared before the Board, and after a ballot had been taken, Dr. Cullen was ap- pointed. Dr. Cullen refurned thanks and assured the Board he would do his work efficiently and well.

DEAR LIVING. A Question About Pensioners. The Guardians of ,the Wandsworth Union asked the Board to support the following reso- lution: "That in view of the cost of living, rents, &c., especially in large towns and cities, having increased by more than 15 per cent. since the old-age pensions came into force, represen- tations be made by this Board to His Majesty's Government with a view to securing a revision of the Act by -which the maximum (55.), at present allowed, be raised to is. 6d. weekly, and the age limit reduced to 65. It was unanimously decided that the letter be left on the table. NURSE'S RESIGNATION, Miss Frances E. Partington, of Runcorn, wrote tendering her resignation as assistant nurse at the Union Infirmary. The resignation was accepted, and the usual course will be adopted for a successor.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====

_ The Local Government Board wrote sanction- ing- the proposal of the Guardians to increase the salaries of Mr. and Mrs. Goddard, superin- tendent and matron of the Children's Home. from £4O to £45 and £3O per annum respec- tively.

Palmer and Mrs. E. Carey were appointed assistant nurses at salaries of .t 2.5 per annum respectively, with the usual allowances.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Costs of County Boundary . Application.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tues- day, Councillor C. E. Linaker presiding.

WESTON MATTERS.

At a meeting Of the Weston Parochial Com- mittee, a letter was read from the Castner- Kellner Company stating that they would be prepared to allow the Council the use of their fire brigade in ease of fire in the parish of Weston, subject to the Council indemnifying the company against all claims in respect of accidents to workmen or damage to the fire engine.—It was resolved that the consideration of the whole question of provision against fire be deferred until the next meeting, and that the Clerk prepare information relating to the cost of insurance of workmen and fire engine, the cost of purchase of fire hose and stand pipe, and as tn the present pressure from the water mains in the parish. The minutes of the committee were approved.

COUNTY BOUNDARY ECHO.

The Local Government Board wrote with reference to the costs incurred by the Runcorn Rural Council in opposing the application of the Lancashire County Council for the alteration of the boundary between the counties of Lancashire and Cheshire. and stated that the Board would not be prepared to make any order. Canon Morley Stevenson on the Advantages of

the Profession.

TRAINING COLLEGE PRIZE DAY.

Whilst the Principal's address at the annual prize distribution in connection with the War- rington Training College on Saturday em- bodied many encouraging statements as to the successes of the students, there was struck one regrettable and discordant note, and on this—the marked decrease in the number of training college students and its effects on the teaching profession—Canon Stevenson spoke at length, pointing out that there were only about 4,000 students to fill the 7,000 places in the training colleges.

Mr. Alfred Watkin,ex-Ifig,li Sheriff of Che- shire, presided, and amongst those on the plat- form were Rev. Canon Stevenson (Principal), Venerable Archdeacon Spooner (honorary secre- tary to the College Council), Rev. College Maintains Its Reputation.' Canon STEVENSON welcomed the relatives and friends of the students who had assembled such numbers. They had, lie said, the oppor- tunity of seeing something, if but fl little, of the college in which the students had spent two years of their life. He was sure they were years upon which the students would look back with many recollections, and he hoped pleasant recollections, as long as they lived. He put it briefly in saying that the College had had a happy and successful year. (Hear, hear.) In the examination which finished CIO last year's work, every student was successful in gaining a Certificate, and about 50 gained distinction in various subjects. The reports received from the inspectors, who had examined the students, were uniformly good, or he might use a stronger word and say excellent. In every respect, the college was living up to its old reputation, and was doing the work which it set forth to do 70 Years ago with a great measure of success. That, of course, was one side, yet a very good side, of the piehire. ire would like tO ex ress P j

in public, as lie had often done in private, the great gratitude he felt to the members of the staff who had worked with him. The successes , of which he had spoken were mainly due to the work of those who assisted him so,loyally and efficiently. (Applause.) Serious Shortage of Students. Last year he had spoken of the great difficulty with which training colleges were face to face, and as that difficulty was still upon them he thought he ought not to pass it by without mentioning it again, and that was the serious shortage of young people who came forward to enter the teaching profession, which meant a serious shortage of students in training colleges. They might think that was a matter for the training colleges, but he could assure them it was going to be a matter for the whole country and the schools of the country. Unless some way could be seen out of the difficulty they would be faced with a very serious shortage of teachers for elementary schools in a year or two's time. In the years 1907-8, 10,340 young people came forward to enter the teaching pro- fession; the next year the number fell to 7,191; the next year 4,813, and last year there were only 4,486, so that in seven years' time the num- ber of people wishing to enter the profession had fallen by 50 per cent., which, of course, was a very serious state of affairs. (Hear, hear.) It was due to various causes, partly because there were other openings for people, especially the Civil Service, and from various causes.which they need not then go into. The position was a very serious one, and, curiously enough, side by side with those figures was an increase in training colleges. Consequently, they had not enough students to fill the training colleges of the country. At the present time there were places for 6,000 or 7,000 students per year. and there were only about 4,000 students to fill the 1 places. That was a serious thing in collegesl from a pecuniary point of view. Colleges were divided into two kinds for that purpose—those who were supported by and took money out of the rates and those who could not, and they, in Warrington, were of the latter. The loss of grant and ep pule rnodmi tout re e . service efees upon each student meant a diminished income, which was not at all ade- quately met by a correspondingly diminished ethz eeFriendstrif n ogt f h oeth eti college,Niloeulcd College h e their b would bestu s tdo a greatt h . to dents fit to do so.

A Reasonable Living Wage. It was quite a reasonably good opening for a girl--he was not going to speak about boys, whose conditions were different—who could start at „t7O to £75 a year, and then increase up to £l2O. That, so long as she was careful not to marry—(laughter)—was a reasonable living wage. If she took the other path and married, she no longer required a living wage. (Laugh- ter.) If she kept to the teaching profession until she reached the age of 60 or 65 years— (laughter)—she was entitled to a pension of ..t5O a year for the rest of her life. (Laughter.) GOT BETTER.

Thin-Buk Proves A Real Home Doctor.

Little Charlie Bidwell, when playing in the house, cut his thumb severely near the nail. "Charlie's thumb swelled alarmingly and the pain went right up his arm," writes Mrs. F. Bidwell, from 166, Thoday-street, Cambridge. "I could see that blood-poison had set in and got very anxious because home-made remedies had no good effect at all.

" I was about to take Charlie to a doctor when I was persuaded to try Zam-Buk. So I got a box of this balm from the chemist, and after dressing the boy's thumb a few times I could see a big improvement. Charlie was re- leased from all his pain and the poison was all drawn away. I kept on using Zam-Buk until the boy's thumb was thoroughly cleansed and healed.

"Zam-Buk also cured eczema on my neck." You cannot do better than get a box of Zam-Buk to-day ko have ready for accidents or any sign of skin disease or soreness. Zam-Buk balm is never sold from door to door, but at the chemists and drug stores. Beware of fraudu- lent and worthless imitations.

WOMAN'S WORLD.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Revival of Coaching. Both the Four-in-Hand Club and the Coaching Club presented a fine show at Ascot. It really seems that road coaching is regaining its lost popularity, for there were more really smart privately owned—not, as often has been the case, jobmasters' teams—at Epsom this year, than at any time since the great boom in motoring set in. Doubtless the Coaching Marathon—one of the popular features of the International Horse Show—has done much to revive this peculiarly British sport, in which women have an enthu- siastic representative in Miss Brocklehurst.

Originality in Balls. The Society and other papers are always prophesying the decadence and ultimate dis- appearance a Fancy Dress Dances, but to judge from the success of the Midnight Ball at the Savoy in aid of the National Institute for the Blind, and the Peace Ball, these functions are only just arriving. The former, indeed, has inaugurated a new era in balls, and serves to show that despite dismal prophecies, fresh ideas in a programme of amusement are still to be found. The Midnight Ball had many new features. It began on the stroke of midnight with a repast, and ended at five a.m. with a hot breakfast. Artistes from the Comedy and Variety stage gave "turns" for the entertain- ment of those who did not dance. The number was limited in order to avoid crowding, and last, but not least, each ticket entitled its holder to a drawing in which there were many valuable prizes.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
French Models.

• Many beautiful dresses were designed and made for Ascot in London workrooms. Yet in the showrooms they have to be labelled. French models, or they would probably not find cus- tomers. Years ago our women had their own styles and fashions, which suited them admir- ably, as may be seen by the pictures of the Great Masters of the seventeenth and eighteenth

centuries. French fashions are designed fer Frenchwomen, and nothing could be more be- coming to them; but Englishwomen are a dif- ferent type, and neither French millinery, noe corsets, nor footwear can be successfully, or even comfortably, worn by the average British woman. Yet everyone. hankers after French modes! The Vogue for Black Velvet. . .

Black velvet plays a very conspicuous part in , this summer's fashions. Hats of it are among the most fashionable of the milliner's confec-, tions, and it is used as revers and cuffs, and with great effect on white pique coats and skirts. In some instances there are outside pockets of velvet also. Sleeves are made of it for outdoor wear, and the latest sunshade for wearing with the white frocks, for which there is such a vogue, is also of velvet. The idea, however, is not altogether new. The velvet sunshade made its first appearance in Paris last year, but was by no means a success; it was considered too heavy for the fragile frocks it accompanied. Perhaps it will meet with a better reception this season. Not much can be said in favour of the fantastic sunshades, with their ridiculously long handles and surprising shapes. The prettiest and most reasonable are flounced, sometimes with three very full volants, edged with narrow black rib- bon velvet put on in a waved design, or num- bers of very narrow flounces cover the shape entirely. The tunic sunshade is a novelty. It has a full, deep frill hanging loosely from the top. Stripes, plaids, and plain bright colours are used for serviceable every-day parasols.

Some Other Sunshades and Pretty Ruffles. Gathered chiffon lines many of the smartest sunshades, and the same material pleated is used for the outside, and finished with a deep border of velvet. Fringe has made its appear- ance on the edge of some of the flat Japanese designs, usually built of taffetas. Black lace butterflies a foot in depth encircle a donieshaped sunshade, also edged with a deep border of black velvet. A very curious deeply domed shape in white satin has black circular lines that give to the whole the appearance of a huge cobweb, and to complete the illusion, a gigantic spider figures at one side. There seems to be no limit to device's for becoming anti Pretty ruffles, many of which could easily be made at

home. Black velvet ribbon is largely employed here also, and with uncurled ostrich feather trimming makes a cool, yet pretty, finish to a summer frock. The velvet goes through the, middle of the feathers, which end just under the ears, the velvet outlining the V corsage, and falling below the opening, where the ends are caught by a pretty brooch or buckle.

Serge in Paris. No material is at the moment enjoying wider popularity in Paris than serge, in fine makes. Formerly it was relegated to the tailor; now the dressmaker has exercised her ingenuity upon it and demonstrated that it can be most success- fully combined with other materials for the confection of outdoor and afternoon frocks of the smartest types. The Rajah tunic is a fav- ourite mode, and with transparent sleeves, white collars and guimpes, or swathed lace collars, these gowns are very suitable for the chilly weather, for which this year has been uncom- fortably remarkable. Parisiennes belonging to the classes that dress with taste truly French rarely appear out of doors in bright colours, and dark blue is a favourite shade. Black and white, or black, are almost an obsession with the true Parisienne, and it must be admitted that she appears at her best in the former, and is an adept in combining the latter effectively. Skirts are unmistakably under, and a new "device that gives freedom of movement is a gusset-like gore, let in at the sides. This Season's Bathing Dresses. ,

The bathing season may be said to have begun and the new dresses are being shown in most of the big stores. Those designed for the women who mean business are comfortable, light, and yet pretty in shape. The simplest, yet quite the smartest of swimming outfits is a black or coloured stockingette one-piece suit, which has a fine serge basque in apron fashion divided at the sides into two pieces, forming aprons in front and at the back. A big square collar, also of serge, is finished at the open neck with a workmanlike sailor tie of black or coloured silk.. Some of these gowns are of spun silk, or they can be had in fine wool. More ornate are the black alpaca dresses in combination style, trimmed with white or coloured braid. Bathing shoes are of white canvas, black satin, and alpaca, and the stockings are often in colour, that contrast with the dresses.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMS. FOR MOTHERS.

Children and Sweets.

All children like sweets and eat them when they can. This is because their system calTs for sugar to keep up the supply of energy that the child is so continuously drawing upon. Sugar he must have, and the only restrictions used should have regard to the quality of the sweets and the time at which they are eaten The child who is allowed to suck sweets at all hours of the day is likely to have no very good appe- tite for plain wholesome food. If on the other hand a•few sweets be given regularly after a meal rip harm will be done. Eaten in excess sweets turn sour in the stomach and set up fermentation, but a moderate amount will. simply supply a little extra nourishment in a very agreeable form.

Wholesome Sweets. The sweets must be wholesome, and in this particular some of the least expensive are the best. Old-fashioned toffee, barley sugar, and the variously named drops flavoured with fruit essences are all .afe enough and contain little beyond sugar. The best way to, guard against the indiscriminate indulgence in sweets is for a mother to keep a supply from which she can give out at proper times every day just as much as she deems wise. There will then be little temptation to spend every halfpenny on sweet- stuff for consumption between meals.

Mould on Books. This can be prevented by placing a few drops of oil of lavender and Canadian balsam in a back corner of each shelf. This will not injure the bindings of leather books as sulphur compounds do, but helps to preserve the bindings.

one way, with a privilege, which, he thought, no other profession had, in such a large measure, and that was holidays. Every Saturday clear off and about eight weeks a year holiday—there were not many professions which could rely on such a privilege. Ile did not say the teachers did not deserve it—(laughter)—because the pro- fession was one which made great demands on the nervous systems of the persons employed in it. Last, but by no means least, it was a pro- fession which enabled one to do a work which was a work beyond all others in the country, which was needed, and which was for the good of the State and its citizens. (Hear, hear.) A teacher who did his or her work well might not only be conscious that be was earning a hard living, but doing a really good work for his country and his God. (Hear, hear, and ap- plause.)

" If you put all these things together," con- cluded the Principal, "I say the teaching pro- fession is one that is worthy of the respect and attention of parents who are looking, about for something for their daughters to do." (Hear, hear, and applause.) FIXITY OF TENURE IN THE PROFESSION. Mr. ALFRED WATKIN, who was given a hearty reception, said that as he saw so many parents present he would like to congratulate them upon the fact that they had sent their daughters to that College. Perhaps some of the parents had made small sacrifices, and those who had done so deserved praise. He was not ashamed to say that he had made sacrifices for his ODM children so that they could have the best education it was possible for him to give them; it was the parents' duty to do that, and, in that College, their daughters undoubtedly received an educa- tion which was all that anybody could possibly require. They all knew that whatever their station in life might be. changes did come, and perhaps misfortunes did overtake them, but if their children were equipped with a thorough education, such as that college gave, they had an asset which no reverse of fortune could take away; they were then equipped for the battle of life, and could fight it against all corners. (Ap- plause.) The young ladies had every reason to be proud of the College. (Applause.) Their lives had certainly fallen in a pleasant place— he did not mean to say that Warrington was the most beautiful place on the face of the earth—(laughter)—although Mr. Rylands might think so—(laughter)—but that College, which was 70 years old, had a reputation far beyond the limits of the town, and was controlled by a Principal, who was recognised as a great author- ity on educational matters—(hear, hear)—who was esteemed lry all who knew him, and had the affection of all his pupils. (Loud applause.) As the Principal had said, he was assisted by a most able and loyal staff, so he did not think that the College could be better equipped. He had been reading the report of the Archbishop's inspector for last year, the concluding remarks of which stated: "The College is, as usual, ex- cellent in all respects. There is no college under abler guidance or doing more faithful work." "Ladies and gentlemen," continued Mr. Wat- kin, "I think that speaks volumes not only for those who teach, but for those who are taught here." ' '

It was true, as Canon Steven- son had said, that there may be other walks in life in which they could have earned, if they referred to money, a larger income, hut, after all, there was more certainty in that profes- sion. COOKERY CORNER.

Some Useful Ilecipes.

To make chocolate cream: Take 1 pint cream (double), 3oz. chocolate, gill milk, 2oz. sugar, loz. gelatine, 1 teaspoonful essence of vanilla. Soak the gelatine in the milk, then melt it. Grate the chocolate and place it in a saucepan, add 2 tablespoonfuls of milk, and cook until the chocolate is quite smooth and a dark colour. Whip up the cream, stir in the choco- late when it is cold, add the gelatine,, sugar, and vanilla. Pour it into an oiled mould, and put in a coo/ place to set. Pineapple Jelly.

Take 1 tin pineapple, 11 pints water, 50z. lump sugar, whites and shells of 2 eggs; allow lioz• gelatine to 1 quart of pulp. Cut pine-apple in small pieces, keep a few pieces to ornament the mould. Pound the remainder in a mortar. Soak the gelatine in 1 gill of the water, place the remainder of water in a saucepan with the sugar and pineapple, and boil gently for twenty minutes; add the soaked gelatine, and allow it to melt, let it cool a little, add the whites and shells, and whisk it until it boils, cover the pan, and strain when it has stood twenty minutes. Fruit Cake.

Use 11b.• flour, 6oz. orange and lemon peel, vb. butter, 20z. citron peel, Vb. sifted sugar, loz. raisins or cherries, Vb. sultanas, Vb. cur- rants, 11 teaspoons ground ginger, 1 teaspoon baking powder, 1 teaspoon spice, Vb. almonds, 1 teaspoon browning. Beat eggs (with yolks) separately. Bake in a greased and papered tin for four hours.

HINTS ON HEALTH. Two Valuable Points.

When children are artificially .fed, the need of some safe laxative is commonly felt after the third or fourth month. The juice of oranges, apples, or grapes may be used, but it must be carefully prepared. The fruit must be perfectly sound, and the juice prepared immediately before it is wanted. It must also be carefully strained through a piece of muslin, to make slre that no fibre of the fruit gets in it. Twice as much water should be added to it, this hav- ing been first boiled. A couple of teaspoonfuls of juice will generally be found sufficient for a very young child, and it should be given about mid-day, and between meals.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
Ringworm.

This is a vegetable organism .which grows in the hair and inflames the soft fleshy substance in the neighbourhood. A cure can only take place by poisoning the organism, and this is done by some irritant substance which at first causes some inflammation of the skin. The following is most useful: Salicylic Acid thirty grains, lodide of Sulphur Ointment one ounce. This should be well rubbed in night and morn- ing for two or three weeks, after which a cure should take place, even on the scalp. On the skin all that is necessary is to paint the affected part with Tincture of lodine a few times, or the above ointment may be used. Headache Cure.

Gentle friction of the scalp of the head is an undoubted method of giving -relief to a nervous head, says Mrs. Orman Cooper, in "Everyday Accidents and Ailments." Let someone gently part the hair and comb it back, passing the fingers along the scalp at the same time. Ilk

ally speaking, the greater the income the greater the risk. However, in their profession there was a certain amount of fixity of tenure. Archdeacon SPOOIVER, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Chairman, said he disagreed with the Principal when he remarked that wheri a young lady entered upon holy matrimony she did not require a living wage. STUDENTS ENTERTAIN THEIR VISITORS.

Immediately after the distribution of prizes, the visitors went out on the terraces and lawns, on one of which several groups of students gave, in succession, a nuraber of country dances. The graceful energy and precision of the dancers called forth loud applause. Tea was then served in the large library and the second year classroom, and as soon as they had been thus refreshed, the visitors spent a pleasant hour in listening to the Band of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire --Regiment, which was playing on the south-west terrace, or in in- vestigating specimens of the students' work. Drawing, basket-making and clay modelling were on view in the art-room, dressed dolls and other specimens of needlework in the history library, models in a variety of materials to illus- trate lessons in geography, history, literature and science in the museum, and four larger models showing life in other lands—Switzerland, Holland, the Sahara and Japan—done by the kindergarten teachers, in the handwork room. All these attracted much attention. The usual evening service in the College chapel was put forward to six o'clock to enable any friends who wished to do so to be present at it.

The rest of the time was spent in dancing on the lawn or in listening to the band. 'Owing to the delightful weather, the day was one of the Pleasantest prize distributions held in the Training College. RELIGIOUS KNOWLEDGE.

, VI/1k

QUALITY OF MILK.

Important Advice to Milk

Sellers.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL DEALER FINED.

An important statement which milk dealers will .do well to bear in mind was made by the Deputy Town Clerk, when, at the Warrington Police Court on Thursday, he appeared to prose- cute a milk dealer for selling milk with a defi- ciency of fat to the extent of 33 per oent. The defendant wa George Wood, 2, WeHeld- street, who, when asked to plead, stated he sold the milk in the same condition as he bought it.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR THE DEALER'S PROTECTION.

Mr. A. T. Hallaway said that on May sth Mr. Flood (the Foods and Drugs Inspector) took a sample of the defendant's milk, and when it was analysed it was found to be 33 per cent, below the standard fixed by the Board of Agriculture, who set forth that genuine milk should contain at least 3 per cent. of fat. Defendant was pleading that he sold the milk in the same con- dition as he bought it, !nit that was not evi- dence. He would like to point out, not for the defendant's sake alone, but for the sake of the whole of the milk sellers in the town, that if they wished to evade the responsibility, there was only one course, and that was to obtain a warranty from the farmer from whom they pur- chased their milk. If they did that and it was proved that they were selling the milk exactly as they bought it, proceedings would be taken against the person who sold them the milk. Aftei. reading a ruling by Mr. Justice Grantham, given in 1890, in the matter, he said that if a milk seller did not take that precaution it was :his own fault. In the case before the Bench there was no evidence to show when the adulter- ation took place, for the defendant only ob- tained the milk about 7.30 a.m., and the Sample was taken about 8.30 a.m.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
DEFENDANT'S STORY.

The defendant said he had sent out a quantity of milk, so that when Mr. Flood took the sample there would only be three or four quarts left. It was impossible for him to take the fat off the milk in the time; in fact, he never interfered with the milk. He acted quite honestly, as he had done for the past 13 years. He had written to the farmer, asking him what he was going to do, hut had received no reply. He did not think anybody in Warrington had a warranty. Defendant handed to the Bench a copy of the letter he had sent to the farmer. The Clerk: You have no warranty?—No. The Clerk: You should see that you have one.

' Mr. Hallaway : It is only fair to say that we took a sample of the farmer's milk .on the fol- lowing day, and it was above the standard. The defendant: He knew you had taken a sample of mine? 1 Mr. Hallaway : Yes; as I said, we took his the following day. The Chairmin (Mr. W. Roberts) said that the evidence showed that defendant was responsible, but they would be as lenient as they could. The full penalty was £2O, but, taking into considera- tion that defendant had never been before the Court previously, they did not wish to deal harshly, although they must record a conviction. He would be fined 205., including costs. Defendant: I do not think it is fair that I should be prosecuted for another man's faults. The Chairman: We have no evidence of that. Defendant: I have complained regularly dur- ing_the last winter, _ _ . _ _

The Chairman: can only deal with the evidence as we have it before us. You get a warranty.

CHESHIRE DISSATISFACTION WITH EDUCATION GRANTS.

At a meeting of the Cheshire Education Com- mittee at Crewe, a report was submitted show- ing the effect which the Departmental Com- mittee's recommendations are likely to have upon the educational finances of the county. The committee estimated that the amount re- ceived under the proposed new regulations 'n Cheshire would be about £1,910, or about id. in the X.

A resolution was passed regretting that the proposed method of allocation of the additional money, so far as county antbcrities were con- cerned, would leave little or nothing for the education authority who were charged with the provision of education, but it would go to the relief of certain local rates without any corre- sponding improvement. The committee also re- gretted to find that no increased assistance whatever was proposed for higher education.

===== 0002601_19140620_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
Wilk No" lOW VOX Nil" WM VOW lisk ea" MODERN ATHENS.

The power for good of the quiet, unostenta- tious reign of the late King George who ruled over the country with sympathy and foresight for forty years, will perhaps be more thoroughly appreciated sfnce his martyrdom. He, together with Queen Olga, often worked for the good of the nation against great obstacles of prejudice, superstition, and lack of understanding. The persistent efforts of the queen for the uplift of women have resulted in many helpful educa- tional institutions, of which the flourishing !Institution for the Employment of Women is an example; the new convict prison for women ! where the inmates are permitted to work at looms and needlework or other industries if they are capable, is another result of her efforts, in a part of the world where such institutions are usually of the lowest grade. There are many charming and umisual pictures of daily life to be seen upon the streets of Athens at all hours of the day. The rows of money-changers, and the fruit-vendors with their donkeys, the prevalence of military uniforms, and the gaiety of the cafes, particularly in the evenings, when the heat of the day has subsided, all add to the attractions, and give the city the title of "Little Paris." But there is an alluring charm in the ancient haunts and natural beauties of Athens and her surroundings which attract the visitor from the more ordinary city life. Perhaps the most imposing natural feature, conspicuous from all points, is the finely shaped peak of Mt. Lycabettus, rising as it 'does in its isolated ! position on the plain. The ascent of this moun- tain (910 feet) as a morning constitutional is one of the necessary initiations of the student of arclueology. The venerable priest in the little chapel of St. George on the summit had, at the ! time of our visit, lived' more than sixty years of his life on the height, descending only at inter- vals during that time for supplies. He ex- pressed the wish that at his death his remains would be allowed to lie there.—From "Modern Athens," by F. S. Darrow, M.A., Ph.D., in "The Theosophical Path."
